SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/career-and-employability-services-intranet/SitePages/CVs.aspx

Career and Employability Services
Creating your CV
What is a CV?
A CV is a one or two page document which shows your skills, achievements, qualifications and experience. CVs vary from country to country, but the information here is to help you make a conventional UK CV. The American English term for CV is ‘resume’, so you may also hear this mentioned. Your CV gives an overview of who you are and what you have to offer an employer.
Why do I need a CV?
- CVs are often requested as part of the application process.
- You will need one if you are making speculative enquiries to organisations to find out if there are any potential opportunities for you.
- Recruitment agencies usually ask for a CV.
- Uploading your CV to jobs websites can make it easier for recruiters to find you and get in touch with opportunities.
- We recommend offering a copy of your CV to employers attending our careers fairs, so make sure you bring plenty of copies with you.
Getting started
If you've not created a CV before, the Build My Career CV Builder is designed to help you, using a step by step approach with advice and information for each section. You can then download this as a word document and make further amendments as required, using the information and advice below as a guide.
Types of CV
There are a variety of CVs used in the UK today, so do your research (or ask us!) to make sure the format you choose is appropriate for the type of job you are applying for, the organisation’s culture, your experience and how you wish to sell yourself to the employer.
Types of CV include:
- Chronological: details your education and work history in reverse date order.
- Skills-based: emphasises the skills you have developed throughout your employment, education, voluntary activities, etc. The details about your employment and education are usually kept to a minimum, with the focus being a larger skills section detailing your transferable skills. Evidence and examples should be included to back these up.
- Hybrid: these CVs combine the above two styles. They feature a more detailed employment and education section, as well as a skills section with evidence and examples.
- Academic: most commonly used in postgraduate applications for research based or lecturing positions. This format places greater emphasis on the subjects you have studied, projects or dissertations undertaken and areas of academic interest and research. Post-doctoral CVs also need to include a summary of research expertise together with a list of publications and conferences attended (if applicable).
- Infographic and Video CVs: in creative industries, an unusual approach can help your application stand out from the crowd. This could be in the form of an infographic CV, using graphic design to present your skills and experience in an interesting way, or a video animated/interactive CV to show off your skills. However, for traditional and non-creative roles, a quirky CV may single you out for the wrong reasons and harm your application, so try and gauge your audience beforehand.
What to include in your CV
Firstly, have a look at our CV guideline template, right. It will give you an idea of what information you should put where, and how a professional CV should look.
There is no rule about what you must include in your CV, but the following points are typically what you need to include in all types of CV:
Personal details:
Do include: your name, address, telephone number, current and most reliable email address. You could also add your LinkedIn account or other social media links if appropriate.
Don't include: a photo, your date of birth, gender, ethnicity, marital status, national insurance number or religion in a UK CV.
Personal Profile:
Not essential, but if you do decide to use one, make sure it adds value to your CV - don’t just use generic buzzwords. Focus on the role applied for and include personal qualities, experience and skills.
These are useful in academic CVs as they can be helpful for highlighting the area of research you want to enter.
Can also be useful in explaining a change of direction in your studies or career if you have a varied work or educational history.
Keep it simple and think about these three key things: 1) Who you are; 2)Your skills and highlights; 3) What you’re looking for.
Education and Qualifications:
Start with university (this is most important so should be given prominence) - include relevant modules and grades; perhaps your dissertation title. If you have not yet graduated, you can give your predicted or expected degree results, e.g. BSc (Hons) Computer Science – expected 2:1. If you have lots of additional qualifications, such as ECDL, PRINCE2, COSHH, Health and Safety, you can include them here or create a separate Additional Qualifications section.
Work backwards starting with the most recent adding the name of your high school and dates for A- Levels, GCSEs and equivalents. You don't have to write the full address of each institution - the city or town will do if it's not obvious from the institution name. Keep it concise - a sentence to cover your subjects and grades is fine.
For overseas qualifications you should indicate the UK equivalence.
Employment History or Work Experience:
Give the name of the business where you worked, your job title and the dates of your employment in reverse date order. Underneath this, detail your responsibilities, duties and skills developed.
You can include any voluntary experience in this section or list it separately if appropriate.
If you have a lot of work experience, you may want to divide this into Relevant Employment Experience and Other Employment Experience sections.
Technical skills
List the skills and knowledge which is specific to the job you are applying for, such as laboratory skills: “I have an excellent knowledge of laboratory techniques including protein analysis, handling human samples, analysis of vitamin C content by titration...”
Think about your experiences academically and through any placements and work experience where you have gained the ability to carry out the required tasks of the role.
These could alternatively be included within Education or Employment History, but don’t repeat information.
Transferrable skills
Match the skills you add to your CV to the job description, rather than producing a generic list.
Provide information about how you gained each skill, such as “My verbal presentation skills have been developed to an excellent standard as a result of delivering weekly seminar presentations to groups of 20+ as part of my degree”.
Try to avoid duplicating information shown elsewhere on your CV.
If you also have Additional Skills such as IT, Languages, First Aid, Driving Licence, etc. you can list them here or in a separate section as appropriate.
Interests, Responsibilities and Achievements:
This is optional, but many employers value it as it can indicate a range of skills and qualities such as teamwork, responsibility and trustworthiness as well as your passions and interests.
Are there any of your personal interests that reflect the job? For example, if you are involved in gaming tournaments and applying for video game design roles, or you volunteer for a cause which has a similar ethos to the organisation you are applying to.
Be specific about your activities - “Football” doesn’t say much, but “Football: Captain of Shearbridge Tornadoes five-a-side team, organised and coordinated fixtures and was responsible for team budget” gives recruiters a much fuller picture and includes extra skills; think about how often you do the activity and the level you have reached.
Include any positions of responsibility you have held, such as equipment officer for the archery team, or social secretary of the pharmacy society, etc.
References:
You can state “references available on request”, but it is acceptable to leave off the references section altogether as they are not generally required at this stage of the recruitment process. You should however include two referees on undergraduate placement CVs.
If your application progresses you will be expected to provide references (usually two), so think about who you could ask as referees and make sure they are happy to provide you with a reference.
Presenting and submitting your CV
Formatting your CV - 10 top tips
1. Two-page CVs are the standard for graduate and internship opportunities, but one page is acceptable for part-time work (longer CVs may be acceptable for academic positions, particularly if you have lots of publications and conferences to list).
2. Use a clearly legible font, e.g. Arial, Calibri, Candara or Gill Sans no smaller than size 11.
3. Bullet points: with short concise sentences, each piece of information has the same value and the same chance of being read; key information may be missed in long paragraphs.
4. Effective formatting: if all your text is the same style it can look bland and unappealing, but the use of bold, italics and underlining can break up text and highlight important details.
5. Clear sub-headings: make things easy to find by clearly labelling them. In your skills section, name the skill first so the reader’s eye is drawn to it, e.g. “Communication: developed active listening and clear communication skills helping a diverse range of customers at Tesco.”
6. Be consistent throughout, with clear dates and locations and all indentations, bullet points, etc. all lined up perfectly.
7. Think carefully about the use of colour – formatting for black and white is the best option when it comes to reproducing your CV.
8. Order your sections so that your relevant skills and experience are prominent, ideally on the first page, to highlight your suitability for the role. Try not split sections over the page break.
9. Think about applicant tracking systems (ATS) - the majority of online CV submissions will be scanned by a programme first, so keep your formatting simple (don’t use boxes, tables or graphics) and include key words relevant to the job to increase your chance of selection (see more in FAQs, below).
10. Check spelling and grammar: mistakes can cause your application to be rejected.
Submitting your CV
Always send a covering letter together with your CV, make sure you have read the submission guidelines and you are sending it how the recruiter wants it.
By email
- When emailing, add your covering letter and CV as attachments, rather than typing the covering letter in the email itself, to ensure both are formatted correctly and are professional looking.
- Keep the email formal, professional and concise when applying for a job. Always follow the employer’s instructions and quote any references they require. You also will need to say that your CV and covering letter are attached.
- Think about your email address - it is best to use a professional sounding one rather than a personal address.
By post
- If sending your CV by post, use good quality white or cream paper, and a high-resolution printer for a professional finish.
- Post early to ensure you meet the closing date.
Example email for submitting your CV

Recipient: mrjohnson@azsolutions.co.uk  
Subject: CV and covering letter for the post of Graduate Data Analyst, reference 112/A

Dear Mr Johnson,

Please find attached my CV and covering letter to apply for the position of Graduate Data Analyst. I would be grateful if you could confirm receipt of this application by return email.

If you have any queries, please do not hesitate to contact me.

Yours sincerely,  
Maria Khan  
Email: maria_khan@businessmail.co.uk  
Phone: 07888 123456

Frequently asked questions about CVs
What are applicant tracking systems and do I need to change my CV for them?
The majority of online CV submissions will be scanned by a Applicant Tracking System (ATS) programme first, so understanding how they work will mean your CV has a better chance of scoring enough to pass to the next stage, where your CV will be read by a (human) recruiter.
Don't worry though, if you follow the advice on this page and are tailoring your CV for each application, then you should already be doing most of the right things:
- Use keywords - reflect the language of the job description to describe your skills and tasks in previous roles - the software will be looking for these key words and phrases to assess your relevance to the role.
- Don't use photos, graphics or tables - the ATS scan may miss valuable information if it can't read the format as plain text.
- Keep formatting simple - bullet points are ok, and keep to a maximum of two fonts. Word documents are preferable but simple PDFs are ok - to give you an idea of whether it is readable try copying and pasting from your PDF into Word or Notepad to see if there are any pasting errors.
- Don't try and trick the system - hiding information as white text doesn't work as it is more likely to confuse the ATS, and as with any application, exaggerations and lies will be found out eventually.
Do I have to produce a new CV for every application?
Yes. If a CV is targeted to the job and organisation, you will significantly increase your chances of getting shortlisted for interview. This doesn't mean you have to start from the beginning every time however, you can have a generic CV which you can use as a basis for each application that you can adapt to match your skills and experience with those on the job description.
Will my CV be read?
In the current job market, employers receive a lot of CVs. Recent surveys suggest that employers may only give a CV an initial 7-to-10 second glance before deciding if they want to read it in more detail or discard it. Although this sounds worrying, if you make sure that your CV is clearly laid out and you demonstrate valuable skills, the employer will want to read it more carefully.
Should I embellish the truth to make my CV stand out?
Definitely not. Any lies on your CV are likely to be found out, which is likely to lead to your dismissal if you get the job. Whether you are offered a job or not, if you are found guilty of making a fraudulent job application you may be placed on the CIFAS Internal Fraud database for six years, which would seriously damage your chances of finding work- see the Graduate Application Fraud Leaflet for the full details.
How should I lay out my CV?
Make sure you read this page for all our hints and tips, but an important point to remember is to place key information at the top of each page to have the most impact, and always start a page with a new section; it is good practice to start and finish individual sections on the same page.
Should I include a photo?
There is no need to include a photo, or your date of birth, gender, ethnicity, marital status, or religion in a UK CV.
Also, do not include a National Insurance number to avoid any online identity theft.
Can I split up my employment history into relevant experience and other work, such as part-time jobs?
Yes- you may want to divide your employment or work experience into sections, such as Relevant Employment Experience, Other Employment Experience or Voluntary Experience depending on what you have to offer. Alternatively, you may want to create a specific section on its own e.g. Industrial Placement, Intercalation Year, Summer Internship/Summer Experience etc. Think about the role and what the recruiter will want to know about the most.
What about other qualifications?
Do include other relevant qualifications e.g. ECDL, PRINCE2, COSHH, Health and Safety, etc.
If you have overseas qualifications, indicate their UK equivalence.
Is it safe to upload my CV to online jobs boards?
Once you upload a CV, anyone who has registered as an employer on that site will have access to your contact details - are you comfortable with that? If so, check the different privacy settings on each job board and take care to select the options that are most appropriate for you.
Some recruiters have targets for signing up graduates and they ‘harvest’ CVs from websites for contact details; you may receive an email about a graduate job that sounds great but turns out not to exist. If you do post your CV on a jobs board, make sure you thoroughly check opportunities that come from recruiters to make sure they are legitimate.
We would recommend that you sign up with a specialist recruitment agency instead. Recruitment agencies have to be GDPR compliant which means they need your permission to share your contact details with a third party organisation.
Finally, remember you should never include your date of birth or national insurance number on a CV and you can list the area you live in rather than your full address.
Next steps and further information
- Quick queries: For students who haven't had their CV checked before, we offer introductory quick queries appointments for initial feedback on your CV. These are available every weekday throughout the year, from 10am-12pm and 1-3pm.
- Appointments with Careers: Once you have been to a drop in session, one of our Career Consultants can make further suggestions for improvement on the content. Reserve your appointment online now
- Build My Career: There is a comprehensive section dedicated to CVs on our Build My Career portal, featuring videos, audio clips and other help and advice, including an extremely useful interactive CV builder.
- Part-time jobs: There are sample CVs specifically for student jobs on our part-time work page
- Information Room: We have a wide range of CV and covering letter resources in our Information Room in Student Central, including specialist reference books (such as the Creative CV Guide, You're Hired! How To Write A Brilliant CV) & DVDs (e.g.Cover Letters - The Write Approach).
- Call in and see us and we will help you access the resources you need.
- Workshops: We also regularly run workshops on a wide range of topics including CVs.

Things to Remember!
Arrive at the exam room early
Students are asked to arrive at the examination venue at least 15 minutes prior to the start of the exam
Late students are permitted to enter the exam up to 15 minutes after the Invigilators have started the exam; students arriving later than 15 minutes will be permitted to enter the examination venue.
Your UB Card
Please do not forget to bring your UB card. This will be required before starting an exam.
And of course a pencil for any MCQ exams

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/exams-intranet/SitePages/Guidance-for-exams.aspx

Exams
Guidance for exams
Guidance for on-campus exams
Your on-campus exams may take place as a traditional 'paper-based' exam in a general teaching room, an 'online/Computerised' one taken via Canvas in a PC cluster room.
The exam on your timetable will specify which room it is taking place in, and will include your seat number.
Other types of exams such as Long/Short Time-Limited, at Distance ones, do not take place on campus.
Before your exam …
Give yourself plenty of time to check your timetable to make sure you know:
- Which room it's in
- And in which building
- The date of the exam
- The start time
- What you need to bring with you
You should arrive at least 15mins before the start time.
If you arrive late by 15mins, or more, you will not be admitted contact your Faculty straight away (see Faculty Support section).
Please note: If you are unable to sit your exam due to unexpected personal difficulties that are outside of your control, or you become unwell during an exam you, may be able to apply for Extenuating Circumstances. Extenuating circumstances is the process you can follow to ask the University to take your personal difficulties into consideration.
Please see our Consideration of Personal Circumstances SharePoint page for further details on this.
What you can and cannot bring into an exam
*** please remember to bring your Student ID card ***
Plus:
- a pencil for MCQ exams
- a pen for writing
- a non-programmable calculator, if required for your exam
Items allowed in on-campus exam rooms:
Only essential items are allowed:
- pens, pencils, ruler, other permitted writing/drawing tools, non-programmable calculator if allowed.
- A clear/transparent pencil case.
- 1 bottle of water - no other food/drink allowed.
- 'Open Book' exams - teaching staff will let you know what you are allowed to bring into the exam with you - no internet access allowed.
Items NOT ALLOWED in on-campus exam rooms:
- All hats / headscarves (except for religious reasons), other headgear including earphones/earplugs (see exception for students with an LSP).
- Tools / blades for sharpening pencils.
- Blank (scrap) paper, graph paper - these and Answer Books will be provided.
- Study notes (unless specified for an Open Exam), textbooks, notes on self.
- Electronic devices: mobile phones, smartwatches, tablets, laptops etc... these must be switched off left in your bag/coat in the designated area except in a PC/computer exam where you will be provided with a brown envelope for you to put your phone insided, to be sealed and kept on your desk.
*** Do not bring valuable items into the exam room with you - all personal belongings will need to be left in a designated area ***.
Learner Support Profile (LSP) Exception
If you have a requirement to use TextHelp (Read&Write software) on a PC, as part of your profile, then please remember to bring along a pair of headphones/earphones if you intend to use the audio facility.

Important information
Where do I need to go for my exam? What do I do once I'm there? When do I need to turn up? How can I access extra help?
There's lots you need to know before heading into your exams, and you have an important role to play in the smooth running of them, so please take time to read the guidance on this site carefully
You can find help and guidance in:
- Guidance for on-campus exams
- Quick Links section

You will receive an email notification on the day of publication to alert you that your personal examination timetable is available to view on Publish as well as the Student Portal.

Scroll down the page (use the hamburger/ellipsis icon when on a mobile device) to view a snapshot of your exam timetable.

Exams
Exams at Other Locations
The University expects all students to take formal examinations here in Bradford. Occasionally circumstances beyond one’s control prevent students from returning to Bradford at the time that the examinations are scheduled due to medical or other extenuating circumstances.
The University has developed a policy for such circumstances to enable students to apply for approval to undertake University examinations (normally Supplementary) away from the University campus, either in other UK locations, or overseas in their home country.
The University's policy is that students will only be granted permission to take University examinations at locations other than Bradford due to the following extenuating circumstances:
- serious illness of the student concerned.
- serious illness of a close relative.
- death of a close relative.
- visa expiry.
Students should contact a venue, this should be the British Council, or a recognised local HE Institution in the location you wish to take your exam(s), to check if they can host the exam(s) for you. Once this has been confirmed by them, you need to complete the application form and return this to the Exams Office with supporting evidence no later than the published deadline dates.
Late applications may be considered.
Exchange students / study abroad programmes
Exchange students who are required to return to their course of study at their home University and/or home students who are going abroad to start a period of study in a host University still need to complete the application form however these will be automatically approved and the fee to the University of Bradford will not apply.
Exchange students should discuss any potential problem with attending examinations at Bradford with the Erasmus Office (Study Abroad Bradford) at the University in the first instance so that further advice and/or support may be provided.
Exchange students will be notified by the Exams team here at Bradford of all arrangements made with the host University.
Fee Information
Costs incurred for the administration required to arrange examinations outside of Bradford are paid by the student. Current fees payable to the University of Bradford are £100 per examination. This charge is payable to the University after the examination has been taken by the candidate.
In addition to the University’s fees, the host institutions e.g British Councils also charge students a local fee for the examination services they provide. These fees are variable, and it is advised that you contact the host institution to arrange payment prior to the examination.
Students who, without prior notice, fail to attend University examinations after arrangements have been made will be charged the full fee by the University.
Deadline Dates for Applications in 2024/25:
- Semester 1: 28 November 2024
- Semester 2: 12 March 2025
- Supplementary: To be confirmed (TBC)
Examination Periods for 2024/25:
- Semester 1: 6 January – 17 January 2025
- Semester 2: 28 April – 9 May 2025
- Supplementary (resits): 21 July – 1 August 2025

Exams
FAQs
Examination FAQs
What happens if I can't attend my exam due to snow or other adverse weather conditions?
During the January exam period the Exams Team will monitor the weather forecast. If there are any changes to exam times or cancellations due to the weather, we will publish them here as well as on Social Media sites.
You should ensure you allow plenty of time to arrive safely for your exams. If you have any concerns or queries, please contact exams@bradford.ac.uk
When will my timetable be published?
View timetable release dates
How will I receive my timetable?
Your personal exam timetable will be available on your student portal on the published release date - you will receive an email letting you know that your timetable is available to view. Please ensure you keep refreshing and checking your emails for changes to the schedule and any updates.
Am I allowed a calculator in my exam?
Guidance relating to the use of calculators in exams can be found on the Information for Students page.
How do I find my exam location?
Please see the Campus maps and Building Lists.
What if I am unable to sit my exam?
If you are unable to sit your exam due to unexpected personal difficulties that are outside of your control, or you become unwell during an exam, you may be able to apply for Extenuating Circumstances. Extenuating circumstances is the process you can follow to ask the University to take your personal difficulties into consideration.
Please see our Consideration of Personal Circumstances SharePoint page for further details.
Can I sit my exam in a location other than Bradford?
Policy and guidance on taking your exams in another location
An exam is missing from my timetable, who do I contact?
Please contact the Exams Team in the first instance.
When will I get my results?
Results are released by the Programme Admin teams. Dates of release should be available on your Canvas module.
Am I entitled to prayer breaks during my exam?
The University of Bradford prides itself on our inclusive learning, teaching and assessment strategy which upholds the key principles of confronting inequality and celebrating diversity. We have made adjustments to enable students to observe religious activities which includes no scheduling of teaching or assessment activities during Friday prayers.
Students are therefore not permitted to leave a scheduled teaching or assessment activity in order to pray. Advice from local Imams, and practice from around the UK HE sector, suggests that students should pray before and after the scheduled activity rather than interrupting the activity in order to pray.
What happens if I am late for my exam?
Students will be permitted entry to an exam up to 15 minutes after the formal start time. Please read the Late Arrival Information for further information.

Timetabling, Attendance and Room Bookings
Booking a room
How to book a room
Resource Booker
The University has the latest cloud-based room booking solution that enables both students and staff to create and manage their own ad-hoc room bookings. Students are encouraged to reserve space that directly relates to their studies, such activities may include group work, private study, exam revision etc. Staff may reserve a space that is required for an event that is part of the academic timetable e.g., team meeting, external event etc.
Resource Booker allows the user to quickly and easily reserve an available university location on any smart device 24/7.
You can:
- View and select an available location appropriate to your requirements
- Amend / cancel bookings previously generated
- Receive automated email booking status confirmations
- Export all personal bookings into an alternative calendar for easy access
- Request an individual recurring booking of up to a maximum of five occurrences.
At the time of your request the booking will be reserved, preventing the location from being booked by another user whilst still awaiting confirmation from a member of our team.

Timetabling, Attendance and Room Bookings
Timetabling FAQ
Who do I ask for help with my timetable?
For help with timetable queries contact your Faculty or Programme Admin. To view your timetable see the Timetable Information section on this SharePoint site.
When will the timetables be released?
Students and staff are able to access their personalised timetables via Publish
Where can I find my timetable?
Individual Staff and Student Timetables – Publish
Students and staff are encouraged to use Publish to view and download individual personalised timetables.
This application allows the user to download to your mobile calendar, with the additional function of enabling change notification, ensuring in the event an essential change is made will be automatically reflected and an email notification received explaining the change.
Note: It is important that notification service must be enabled within the Profile section of the Publish application.
How do I access online sessions?
Links to any online sessions will be emailed to you at your University email account - this information may also be provided on Canvas, the University's VLE (Virtual Learning Environment) - available via the Student Portal
Will the timetable change after it's been published?
Any necessary changes made to the published timetable will be updated automatically on online timetables - please make sure you refresh your browser.
Staff and students are responsible for checking their timetable regularly for amendments.
What happens if I'm taught in a room without a card reader?
Most teaching rooms will have a card reader fixed to the wall, close to the entrance. Large teaching rooms may have more than one reader - it doesn't matter which one you use as long as it is in the room where your session is being held.
Some very small teaching rooms may not have a card reader on the wall.
In this situation, your tutor should have a paper Check-In record for to sign.
Can I book a room for a student society meeting, group study or revision?
To book a room use the online room booking service Resource Booker
Will I be able to book the room that I want?
This will depend on availability / suitability / capacity, amongst other things.
With regard to booking teaching rooms during teaching hours / weeks, Faculty requirements for timetabling take priority. However once timetables have been published, others are able to request teaching rooms for non-teaching activities.

When using a University Location
Location Usage Etiquette
University space is there for everyone to enjoy and make full use of the facilities to support your particular requirements. We have a duty of care to consider your well-being when using a space and have such arranged planned cleaning throughout each day in every room. Unfortunately, there has been a number of reports that some locations are not being used for their intended use or used without prior permission.
Could both staff and students ensure a space is first reserved before entering and all space is used respectfully and left in a condition you might expect when you arrive to a planned event.

Room Usage Etiquette
Room Usage Etiquette
As we begin the start of the new term, we would like to ask both staff and students when using location resources you consider how the space is being used. Although we fully encourage the use of all spaces, we ask when using any location that you ensure furniture is not removed from an additional location to support your requirements and ask that you ensure an appropriate space is reserved for your activity.
In addition should any movement of furniture within your room be required that the room is reset after each use and that all belongings are removed and the space is left ready for the next user.

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/placements-office/SitePages/fohs-travel.aspx

Placements Office
Travel and Dual Accommodation Expenses (NHS Learning Support Fund) Guidance
During your programme of study you may undertake a range of placement experiences. Depending on your programme, the placements you undertake will reflect the diverse population that the NHS and Private and Independent sector serves. Placements span across the Yorkshire region and depending upon your programme of study, these may on occasion even be outside of Yorkshire too.
No allowance can be given with regards to travel distance / time, where you live, your method of transport or your family commitments, for example childcare or dependent responsibilities. Whilst on placement you will also be required to reflect the working hours of your placement area so will undertake a variety of shift patterns including early mornings, late evenings, night shifts and weekends during the placement.
Travel and Dual Accommodation Expenses (NHS Learning Support Fund)
To be eligible to claim practice placement expenses the basic rule is that it must cost more to go to placement than it does to travel to university. NHS Learning Support Fund rules state that you should use the cheapest form of transport available.
To claim practice placement expenses, you need to first sign up to a Learning Support Fund account and then download their Travel and Dual Accommodation Expenses (TDAE) form. Please complete this form on a day-by-day basis and if using public transport please provide tickets as evidence with your claim form. If you drive to placement, you do not need to provide petrol receipts, it is your responsibility to ensure that you have adequate insurance cover for all risks associated with its use. Students travelling to and from their placement site in their own vehicle do so at their own risk.
Mileage cannot be claimed if a family member or partner drives the student to and/or from practice placements.
Car parking and tunnel / bridge tolls can be claimed back if the cost together with the mileage is more than travelling to university. Attach proof of payment and parking tickets to your claim form. Some hospitals have parking permits for students, please enquire at the applicable hospital for more information. Please also be aware that car parking cannot be reimbursed where mileage is not being claimed.
NHS funded students must use the most direct route available when travelling to and from their placement.
Car hire
You can claim any excess cost to you of hiring the car, including the appropriate mileage rate and the cost of any car parking/tunnel tolls. Your university must agree and authorise any use of a hire car in advance.
Taxis
Costs incurred from the use of private hire vehicles such as taxis are not covered under this Scheme and students will only be reimbursed for the cheapest method of transport available to them.
Overseas placement costs
You can claim expenses for some overseas placement costs, the TDAE guidance clarifies what costs you can be reimbursed for.
Submitting your completed TDAE form
Please note LSF are launching a new online portal for travel claims on 7 January 2025. The placements admin team can accept paper claims until 7 Feburary 2025.

Placements Office
InPlace
The Placements Administration Team uses Quantum IT's InPlace Placement Management System. This supports our strategic aspirations in relation to placements and helps us to support the delivery of high-quality placements in our professional programmes.
What is InPlace?
InPlace is a single, fully integrated web-based placement administration system. The Placement Administration and Careers and Employability teams use this system to administer and track your placements.
How to access InPlace
InPlace is web-based and prefers Google Chrome or Firefox. Once you access the webpage you should be logged into InPlace, or if you're not signed in, you'll be taken to the University’s logon the same as you would do for Canvas and other University systems.

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/placements-office/SitePages/occupational-health.aspx

Placements Office
Occupational Health
The University must ensure that our students are deemed fit to go on placement. To that end we work with a company called Industrial Diagnostics Company (IDC) (part of the Latus Group) to arrange appropriate health questionnaires, vaccinations or Occupational Health follow-up consultations for the following courses:
- Nursing;
- Midwifery;
- Occupational Therapy;
- Physiotherapy;
- Sports Rehab;
- Diagnostic Radiography;
- Paramedic Science;
- Physician Associates;
- Clinical Sciences;
- Healthcare Sciences;
- Trainee Nursing Associates;
- Pharmacy - health questionnaire/vaccination evidence;
- Social Work - health questionnaire;
These processes are in place to ensure both student and patient safety whilst you are on placement. It is your responsibility to ensure that you keep up-to-date with your appointments - if you don't, you may well be prevented from attending placement.
Vaccination evidence
If you have any questions about your personal vaccination records or if you need vaccination evidence to give to your employer, please contact the Occupational Health Provider (Industrial Diagnostics Company) ohbradfordstudent@latusgroup.co.uk or telephone 01530 239190
Vaccination questionnaire and the importance of attending appointments
Before you attend your first vaccination appointment with the Nurses, you should already have completed the Occupational Health questionnaire and uploaded details of your previous vaccinations. This will allow the Nurses to update your profile and ensure you are given the correct vaccinations. A link to the questionnaire is sent to your personal email account during the summer before you enrol at university. If you cannot find the link to the questionnaire, please contact foh-placements@bradford.ac.uk as soon as possible.
If you fail to attend the appointment that has been organised for you, you risk not being able to start placement at the same time as your colleagues. A valid reason for not attending is if you are ill. You must let us know in advance if you cannot attend.
If you continue to miss appointments that have been organised for you, you will be referred to the academic programme team.
Blood tests
Any student who is unable to provide evidence of their previous vaccinations will be required to have a blood test at the 1st appointment. Paramedic and Midwifery students will also be required to have a blood test at their 1st appointment. All students are required to have a blood test at their 4th appointment to confirm immunity levels. It is really important that you drink lots of water and are well hydrated before you attend the blood test as this will make it easier to obtain a sample.
Vaccination dates
The dates of the 4 x vaccination sessions for your course will be posted on CANVAS at the start of the academic year so please do make a note of these and ensure you are available on those dates. Industrial Diagnostics will email you direct with the exact date/time of your first appointment. This email will be sent from no-reply@ohims.co.uk so please do look out for it. If you cannot find the email from IDC, it is worth checking your junkmail folder. Subsequent appointments will be made when you attend your appointment. Industrial Diagnostics organise hundreds of student vaccination appointments throughout the year and we need you to turn up on the planned dates/time for these to run smoothly.
Vaccination appointments will be sent to your personal email address in the first instance so please check your emails regularly as it is essential you attend all appointments you are invited to. Please do not ask your GP to administer any vaccinations you may need.
Ongoing safety arrangements when attending an appointment
To ensure the ongoing safety of our students and staff, we continue to ensure that all appointments are carried out on a strict social distancing basis. Please adhere to the following requirements:
- please don't arrive early for your appointment - only arrive at the time you have been given in the email invitation;
- please don't gather in groups or with your friends whilst waiting for your appointment;
- you should wait in the corridor outside the Occupational Health suite of rooms (Richmond Building E86-88) until a Nurse comes to collect you;
- you should ensure that you use hand sanitiser both before and immediately after your appointment.
Student feedback on any Occupational Health issue
Please take the time to complete the questions below if you have any issues/problems relating to the organisation and/or administration of your Occupational Health appointment at the University. This could relate to not receiving an appointment, any issue during the appointment itself or turning up to an appointment and then being turned away by the Nurses, due to a vaccine shortage or similar.
It is really important that you let us know of any difficulties that you have experienced so that we can work with all parties to minimise the risks of the situation happening again in the future.
Thank you for taking the time to complete this form which can be accessed here: https://forms.office.com/Pages/ResponsePage.aspx?id=0aJeJGVDJkeAWmMeuT2Tquk3D1jJ2spAj07TqARh5VNUMUNIMjRUTzdLVVY3ME04M1VJUk5ZSElHRS4u 
Change of health during studies
If students develops a new health issue or there is change to an existing health issue during their studies and this has an impact to their studies or placement, it may be appropriate to refer them to our Occupational Health provider, Industrial Diagnostics. Personal Academic Tutors should meet with their tutee to discuss the health issue and discuss the reason for referral with the Programme/Placement Lead before submitting the referral. Please ensure that the initial referral contains a good level of detail and specific questions have been asked. This will ensure that the report is likely to be more detailed and contain practical advice.
Contact the Placements Administration Team if you have any questions regarding any of these processes.
If academic staff feel that the OH report received does not answer specific questions, please do raise this with the Occupational Health provider in the first instance so that further information can be requested from the clinicians.

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/placements-office/SitePages/PARE.aspx

Placements Office
PARE (Practice Assessment Record & Evaluation)
What is PARE
PARE is an online website which provides the following information/support for students undertaking clinical placements.
- Placement Details - staff contact details, basic information about the placement area
- Placement Evaluation - students are required to evaluate placements via PARE
- Online Portfolio - is used for some courses
How to access PARE
Access PARE via https://onlinepare.net/
Student Guidance
- PARE Access - the Placements Administration team add student details to PARE when you enrol on the programme (in year 1). You will receive an email telling you to activate your account.
- University email - ensure you use your exact university email address including all punctuation eg a.n.another@bradford.ac.uk.
- Searching for a Placement Details - use the Search Placements area to find contact details such as a number or an email address.
- Student/Learner PARE User Guide - you can access this via PARE User Guide section on the PARE website.
- Placement Reference in PARE (PR code) usually matches the InPlace Placement (Agency reference) code, so you can search for placement information in PARE using the InPlace reference.
- Placements on PARE - the Placements Admin team add placements to PARE within the first few weeks of placement starting.
- If your placement isn't showing on PARE, it is easy to add this on, see the Frequently Asked section below for guidance.
- Once your placement has finished you have 2 weeks to complete the evaluation
Frequently Asked Questions
My Placement isn't showing and I need to do my evaluation
If your placement isn’t showing on the 'my placement' screen. You will need to refer to the PARE dashboard and search for your placement. This is how you will be able to find contact details such as a number or an email address.
- If your placement has not been added to PARE for any reason, it is easy to add this on 
1. Click on the Evaluation tab > Click on Add NET survey at the top right-hand side; 
2. Search/select the placement provider; 
3. Add the start and end date of your placement; 
4. Select Save 
5. Complete the evaluation once your placement has ended.

You will receive an email from Simon Jersey explaining how to order your uniform. You have 48 hours once you receive this email to set up your login details and order your uniform, after which point the link will expire. Email Simon Jersey NHSUniversity@simonjersey.com if you need a new link to be sent.
Your uniform will be delivered direct to your home address. Please it try on immediately to ensure it fits. If it doesn't fit, please return it to Simon Jersey within 14 days (please do not bring it into university).

Hijab’s should be washed daily at 60 degrees along with other uniform so suitable fabric should be worn to assist this.

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/placements-office/SitePages/Self-Source-Placements.aspx

Placements Office
Self-sourced placements
The University runs many placements on a ‘self-sourced’ basis as part of or an option on many courses. These are placements where the student finds and initially secures the placement themselves like Industrial Placement years, Chemistry, Criminology / Sociology short placements, Public Health and Well Being or Archaeology and Forensic Science placements.
Pre-placement
Your Course Academics will initially guide you in what the requirements of your placements will be, from the timings and length to learning outcomes and they will often be able to steer you to companies and opportunities already out there.
Careers and Employability will be able to guide you in approaching companies, working on your CV and interview skills. We would strongly advise that you contact them as soon as possible as it can take time to secure a placement.
DBS check
If you are undertaking a placement with children or vulnerable adults it is likely you'll need to undertake a DBS check. Please complete this form to request this. Please refer to the DBS guidance for further information.
Secured placement
Once your have secured a placement you must inform the University to gain placement approval. Placements are approved via the University’s Placement system, InPlace. (InPlace guidance is below). Follow the guidance under "Submitting an application for a self-sourced placement.
Information Required for Approval:
- Placement start and end dates (sandwich placements should commence from Mid July, after the exam board).
- Placement Provider contact details, including Organisation Name, Contact name, contact details
- Job Description & Offer Letter
- Salary Details
We require additional information for some courses eg some students must upload an employer agreement/health and safety form. Mandatory fields or information required have a red asterisk. Access InPlace to check what information is compulsory for you.
Once you have submitted your self placement submission the Placement Administration team will check with your course academics for approval and if so we will then contact the placement as the University needs to have received an Agreement and Health and Safety forms back from the company before the placement can be approved.
You should be able to see feedback comments advising you of what's happening with your placement by logging into InPlace and looking at your Notification screen.
Once your placement has been approved you will receive an email from the Placement Administration Team and your InPlace record will clearly show a placement.
If you are completing an Industrial Placement Year then it is vital you check with your Programme Administration Team that you are on the correct version of the Course.
On Placement
While on placement please ensure you maintain professionalism, placements are an excellent opportunity to make contacts in the working world who may be invaluable when the time comes to seek employment.
Absences - record any absences via form. You must also inform your employer and if you're experiencing any long term health issues ie longer than one week, please contact your Personal Tutor/Placement Lead.
Ensure you follow any requirements for your placement as laid out by your course academics and meet any contact points they have arranged.
Support during Placement
As you are registered as a student during your placement you continue to receive support from the University. Your PAT/Placement Lead/Placement Administration Team / Other Academic staff on your programme and Student Life and Wellbeing Services can support you with any issues you are experiencing. So please do not hesitate to ask for help.
Returning from Placement
Before leaving a placement ensure that you return any equipment they may have loaned you for the placement and also ensure you have not left any of your belongings with them.
Your placement may well form part of an assignment or assessment so ensure your have everything you need to enable you to complete this.

Placements Office
DBS
Many courses involving placements require you to have a University of Bradford Disclosure and Barring Service (DBS) check. New starters on the majority of these courses will receive an email asking them to complete the online DBS form, the ID used must then be verified by the My Bradford team. If you have not received an email with a link to the online form please email admissions@bradford.ac.uk during the applications cycle or foh-placements@bradford.ac.uk if you have started the course.
Identification Validation
Please complete the online application form as soon as you are asked to do so. Once you have completed the form, book an appointment with MyBradford to verify your ID with MyBradford. You can book an appointment to verify your ID with MyBradford.
Track your DBS application
We advise you to track your DBS application form after you have completed your ID check. Track this via the DBS website.
If you don't receive your DBS certificate you can request a duplicate copy but this must be done within 3 months.
DBS Update Service
We advise you to register for the DBS update service which will speed up any job offers in the future.
Keep your certificate safe - placement providers may ask to see it and if you lose your DBS certificate, you will need to apply for another for which you will be charged.
Online store
If you are a Registered Student and have misplaced your original certificate and therefore need to complete the application process again, please use the Online store to apply for a DBS check. Please note there will be a charge for this.
International Students
International students who are enrolled onto the course with a Police Check usually complete a DBS during Year 1 Semester 2. Communications are usually sent in December advising students what types of ID can be used. The DBS check is usually launched in January/February. If you have any questions or need any support please contact the Placement Admin team.

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/placements-office/SitePages/travel.aspx

Placements Office
Travel and Accommodation Guidance
NHS Placements Learning Support Fund
General Travel Guidance
During your programme of study you may undertake a range of placement experiences. Depending on your programme placement span across the Yorkshire region and can also be outside Yorkshire too.
No allowance will be made with regards to maximum or minimum travel distance / time of where you live, your method of transport or your family commitments, for example childcare or dependent responsibilities. Whilst on placement you will also be required to reflect the working hours of your placement area which may involve a variety of shift patterns including early mornings, late evenings, night shifts and weekends during the placement.
Driving
Before attempting to use your car to travel to and from placement please ensure that you have adequate insurance to cover all risks associated with your vehicle, that your vehicle is roadworthy and has a current MOT Certificate if required. Please check with your car insurer about insuring your car for business use if necessary, some placement providers may require business insurance. Under no circumstances are you allowed to transport patients as per Trust / Placement policies.
It is advisable to plan your route and parking options prior to commencing placement and try and pre-empt the traffic levels. There are links to some route planners in the Useful Travel Websites section.
Public transport
It is advisable to pre-plan your journey and work out which buses/trains you will need to access prior to commencement of placement and also any necessary walking or cycling routes.
Hospital shuttle buses
Many Health or Life Sciences placements occur in hospitals, some hospitals may operate shuttle buses. Please check with reception at your placement area or website about shuttle buses.
Bicycle hire
Bicycles can be hired or bought second hand from Bradford Cycle Hub.
Adverse weather conditions
In the event of adverse weather conditions, please use your own judgement to gauge whether it is safe to travel to your placement and back. Bear in mind that in heavy snow or flooding sometimes buses and trains can be cancelled or stop running altogether.
Accommodation
Students may benefit from staying in accommodation such as a hotel or Bed and Breakfast if the travel distance is lengthy. Some hospitals offer accommodation; please contact FOH-Placements@bradford.ac.uk for contact details. NHS funded students may be able to claim this back up to the value of £82.50 per night.
Contact details for accommodation at Airedale General Hospital and Bradford Royal Infirmary are below.
Airedale - 4 bed shared unit - self contained bedroom and bathroom with communal kitchen/living room. Bond of £450 required which is fully refundable on departure providing no damage. Contact Allison Crocker Allison.crocker@nhs.net for availability.
Bradford Royal Infirmary - Lynn Hadley Accommodation Supervisor – 01274 364102
Short term loans for placement expenses
If you experience financial difficulty with upfront placement travel or accommodation costs for placement you can receive a short term loan from the University. Please read the Canvas guidance and complete the Student Support Fund Load Application Form. You must complete the first section (personal information) and section B relating to placement costs.
If you have any questions about the loan or agreement please contact supportfundloan@bradford.ac.uk

TROUBLESHOOTING
I’m a new student who hasn’t received my email invite to eLearning for Health
Programme Admin will be sending a list to eLearning for Health around your induction week and you should receive notification they have requested your logon. The email from eLearning for Health should then arrive within a few days.
I’ve forgotten my password
Go to https://www.e-lfh.org.uk . Click register / Log in (top right hand corner) then click on the Forgotten password link. Enter your Username to reset your password.
I’ve forgotten my username
Go to https://www.e-lfh.org.uk . Click register / Log in (top right hand corner) then click on the Forgotten username link. Then enter your university email address in the ‘forgotten username’ field. eLearning for Health use your full University email address, which contains dots - for example: j.r.bloggs2@bradford.ac.uk (note the dots). This is not the same as the logon you would use to access canvas and other University systems (in the example above, the logon would jrblogg2@bradford.ac.uk).
I’ve tried the forgot username link but it says my email is invalid
Are you using your full University email address? Please remember that eLearning for Health use your full University email address, which contains dots - for example: j.r.bloggs2@bradford.ac.uk (note the dots).
This is not the same as the logon you would use to access canvas and other University systems (in the example above, the logon would jrblogg2@bradford.ac.uk).
I've completed a module several times but it does not show as passed or completed
eLearning for Health: "Depending on the computer system and the web browser used to access our learning, communication between the content object and the learning management system can occasionally not occur for a myriad of different reasons. This can result in completions and scores not updating correctly on a user’s profile. Due to the many different computer systems and web browsers that are currently used, the way in which they can all be configured, updated and changed from the default standard, and the many different types of content we currently provide, it is challenging to confirm an absolute correct configuration to access our learning and always achieve correct completion and score updates.
However, we do recognise how frustrating it can be to spend valuable time progressing through a learning object only to have your completion or score not recorded. If this does occur then we sincerely apologise.
We have put together the following information to help you hopefully avoid such an event occurring again.
Whether you are using Windows, Macs, home computers, laptops, trust computers, Android devices, IPhones and IPads or any other device, there are web browsers that can be installed on them to access internet sites. The main web browsers are Google Chrome, Firefox, Safari, Internet Explorer 11 and Microsoft Edge. In their default format, they should all work fine with our site. If you have installed add-ons into them then they can sometimes reduce the functionality of our site.
1. First and foremost, could you run our Technical Checker, to check for any possible browser configuration issues: [
https://portal.e-lfh.org.uk/TechnicalChecker 
2. Please make sure any ad-blocking add-ons are disabled for our site. We do not use adverts in any of our content, but some of our animations can be snagged by overzealous ad-blockers. Please add our site domain to the allowed list.
3. Please ensure pop-up blockers are configured to allow our site to use them. Most, if not all, of our learning objects open in a browser window.
4. If you are using a web browser and completion and/or scores are not recording, try installing one of the other web browsers and attempt the same piece of content again. This can help us work out if the content is not functioning correctly, as it is doubtful that it would fail in two different browsers at the same time unless it is badly configured.
5. We strongly recommend you use the latest version of an alternative browser - Internet Explorer 11, Firefox, Edge, Chrome, or Safari - these are the only browsers which we support for use with our e-Learning.
6. You may be struggling to access content from a trust computer but you could attempt to access from your mobile device or home computer instead. This would confirm if the issues is site based or at the trust/local location.
7. Make sure the content you are trying to complete has been progressed through correctly. Sometimes questions can get missed and this can affect the overall completion of the content.
8. Please make sure, once you have completed a session, that you close the e-Learning window correctly by clicking on the 'X' at the top of the e-Learning window (red dot on a Mac). If using a tablet or smartphone, ensure the e-Learning is not left running in the background
Please note: We cannot manually manipulate any user’s learning history on our site. If a learning object has not recorded as complete, or updated a score when you think it should have, then you must attempt it again after you have made any necessary changes as outlined in the above information. Sending us screen shots of completed learning objects may prove that you have indeed obtained completion or a certain score but it doesn’t enable us to add it to your user profile."

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/campusconnect-intranet/SitePages/Do-you-receive-verification-codes-(for-MFA)-via-text-message-.aspx

IT Services
Do you receive verification codes (for MFA) via text message?
The information below applies to you if you receive verification codes via text message when approving your sign-ins to University services.
Microsoft is making a change to the 'text message' method for MFA (multi factor authentication).
This change means that, instead of sending verification codes via standard text messages, Microsoft will attempt to send the codes via WhatsApp
Note: This only applies if you have WhatsApp linked to the same phone number that receives the text messages. If you don't have WhatsApp associated with your phone number, you'll continue to receive codes via text message - the same applies in the event that your device has no internet connectivity.
The first time you receive a verification code in WhatsApp, Microsoft will notify you (via a text message) that the code has been sent to your WhatsApp.
The messages sent to WhatsApp are similar to the text messages but contain more Microsoft branding and a verified checkmark.
Please note that the phone number associated with Microsoft's WhatsApp Business Agent is: +1 (217) 302 1989. You can verify that the message originates from that number by selecting 'Microsoft' at the top of the message (the Business Agent number is then displayed).
Important
Regardless of how you receive MFA verification codes, ensure you are expecting to receive a code (that is, shortly after you’ve requested access to a University service, and not at any random times). Always remain mindful that phishing attempts can come not just via email, but also via text messages, social media platforms, QR codes, and other methods. If you are suspicious of any messages, don’t act on them - please report them
What is the University's recommended method for approving sign-ins?
The recommended and most secure method for approving sign-ins is via the Microsoft Authenticator app (feedback also suggests this is the easiest method).
Therefore, if you have a smartphone, we encourage you to install the Microsoft Authenticator app and set that as your primary authentication method (you can learn how to add the Authenticator, and change your default method, in the MFA guide).

- discrimination
- sexual misconduct
- hate
- other behaviour that falls short of what we expect from students and staff at the University.
Reports can be made anonymously or with your details, that is completely up to you.
Dignity and Respect Advice Service
Dignity and respect
Our staff and students expect to be treated with dignity and respect, and the University is a place where people are protected from treatment which demeans them, or is unfair or intimidating.
The University of Bradford is fully committed to sustaining a positive and mutually supportive working environment free from harassment, discrimination, bullying and victimisation where students and staff can work collaboratively and productively together, and where everyone is equally valued and respected.
What you can do
Please remember your own responsibilities to treat your colleagues, students and all guests to the University with the dignity and respect you expect to receive yourself. You can play your part by being sensitive to the views of others, which may frequently differ from your own, and trying to create an environment where your own behaviour, language or conduct does not create any offence or misunderstanding.
It is the responsibility of every member of the University to help create an inclusive community where everyone is enabled to achieve their full potential and mutual respect is the norm.
More information
Further information around dignity and respect can be found in the policy and guidance documents, on the People, Culture and Wellbeing SharePoint.
If you would like to speak to someone from CfID about this service, please contact equality@bradford.ac.uk or the Dignity and Respect Manager Harnek Panesar.

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/centre-for-inclusion-and-diversity-intranet/SitePages/Pronouns-in-email-signatures-and-beyond-–-a-brief-guide.aspx

Centre for Inclusion & Diversity (CfID)
Pronouns in email signatures and beyond – a brief guide
The use of pronouns in email signatures, name badges and social media is becoming more commonplace in professional environments. The NHS incorporated pronouns in its #hellowmynameis campaign in 2020. The APPG LGBT+ (All-Party Parliamentary Group on Global LGBT+ Rights) highlighted their importance the same year. In 2021, LinkedIn introduced it as a separate field on profiles.
What’s a pronoun and why is it important?
In this context, pronouns are what we use to refer to someone instead of using their name, for example “he” or “she”. Where we might not know someone’s gender, we may refer to them with a singular “they”. Getting someone’s pronoun wrong can be embarrassing, but it can also reveal hidden biases – we know not all doctors are “he” and not all nurses are “she”, but our brains may still default to one or the other. We can also make assumptions on pronouns based on name or appearance, even though a Chris might be a woman and a toddler in pink dungarees could be a boy. In all cases, if we get it wrong, we quickly apologise and move on, making sure we get it right next time.
Culturally, getting someone’s pronouns correct, and by extension their gender, is important. Accidentally misgendering someone is considered largely awkward, but forgivable. Doing it purposefully or repeatedly is perceived as dismissive, aggressive or openly hostile.
Trans people and pronouns
Pronouns are especially important to someone who has undergone a gender transition, as they will have strong feelings about their gender identity. The University employs a number of trans staff, with over 1,000 across UK Higher Education providers self-identifying as under the trans umbrella. See more information on the Advance HE site
Over 13,000 students nationally told us in 2018/19 that their gender identity was not the same as what was assigned to them at birth. These staff and students may have already undergone transition or they may be undergoing it right now, in which case their pronouns can change after you meet them. Instead of a trans person having to have awkward conversations with everyone around them, it’s best to simply have pronouns somewhere for others to see.
Much like someone changing their name after marriage or divorce can take a bit of getting used to, all that’s expected if someone is transitioning is to make a note of their new name and pronouns and address them correctly going forward.
Beyond he & she
In the 2019/20 academic year, 595 members of university staff and 3,865 university students across the UK identified as neither male not female. This can mean different things for different people, for example that they are non-binary, genderfluid or agender
While the UK does not officially recognise genders beyond male & female (for example on passports), many organisations linked to the Higher Education sector do. Besides pronouns, this is also reflected in the use of titles like Mx (a gender neutral version of Mr or Ms). Some countries, including Canada, Iceland, Australia and Denmark, do officially recognise a third gender on passports, which is broadly (though not exclusively) associated with non-binary people. More countries are in the process of standardising a third gender identifier.
What does it mean when someone mixes pronouns?
When LinkedIn introduced pronouns in profiles, they allowed for custom options. According to them, these are the five most common custom pronouns on their platform.
In the blog post, LinkedIn provide some explanations from users themselves on why they have chosen custom pronouns. There is no simple answer, but some reasons may be that the person is, for instance, non-binary but presents as masculine or feminine, that they are still exploring their identity or that they are happy in both or any identities. In all cases, it means the person can be referred to by either given pronoun, though they may have a stronger preference for one or the other, reflected in the order given.
These pronouns don’t make sense!
Much like coming across a name you’ve never seen before, you may encounter some pronouns that are unfamiliar. These have been created by communities who may not have felt represented by existing pronouns or created in solidarity with people whose language does not allow for gender neutrality (Greek and Spanish are such languages). We refer to these as neopronouns.
Some more commonly used examples:
- He / She / They
- Him / Her / Them
- His / Her / Their
- His / Hers / Their
- Himself / Herself / Themselves
- Xe / Xem / Xyr / Xyrs / Xemself
- Ve / Ver / Vis / Vis / Verself
- Per / Per / Pers / Pers / Perself
- Ze / Zir or Hir / Zir or Hir / Zirs or Hirs / Zirself or Hirself
- Ey / Em / Eir / Eirs / Emself
Don’t worry, they are new to most people. They also evolve so the best thing is to check what pronouns someone actually uses and go with that. If you are really committed however, there is an app for that
If you are unsure how to pronounce them, do what you’d do when encountering a new name: either wait to hear someone else say it, ask people who know the person better or ask the person directly!
Do I have to include my pronouns in my signature?
No. You will have your own reasons for this. Perhaps you’re not entirely sure what your pronouns are or perhaps you’re in the process of transitioning, but are not out to everyone around you. Whatever your reason, it’s OK to not include pronouns in your signature. However, if you don’t have a specific reason not to, then you are very strongly encouraged to. It normalises their use which creates a more inclusive, welcoming and safe space for everyone, reflects our University values and shows trans and non-binary people that you are an ally. Importantly, no matter how strong your own sense of gender identity, assuming that everyone else could guess it can be problematic, as they may not want to make any assumptions!
While the use of pronouns has been associated strongly with trans inclusion, the bottom line is that someone’s name, appearance or job title will not necessarily reflect their gender. The only way to be sure is to ask or let that person tell you. Including pronouns in a signature, a name badge or a profile is a quick and easy way to do just that!
Example
For uniformity, pronouns should come immediately after the name in either the second line or in parenthesis immediately after the name in the signature.
Professor Uduak Archibong MBE, PhD, FRCN, FWACN
She / Her / Hers
Pro Vice-Chancellor [Equality, Diversity and Inclusion] Professor of Diversity
Or
Professor Uduak Archibong (She / Her / Hers)
Pro Vice-Chancellor [Equality, Diversity and Inclusion]
Professor of Diversity
Note: You don’t have to use all three pronouns in your signature. Most people prefer just two – She / Her or He / Him or They / Them.
Is this really that important to some people?
Yes.
Anything else I should know?
The third Wednesday of October is International Pronouns Day! You’re welcome to participate in this event.

Centre for Inclusion & Diversity (CfID)
n-Able: Network for Promoting Disability Equality
About the Network
n-able meet four times a year in an informal setting to discuss issues, initiatives and support within the disability agenda. We also organise events and activities.
The network aims to:
- Provide a forum for disabled staff to:
  -- Share knowledge and experience in a safe and supportive environment.
  -- Have a collective voice and contribute to the University’s disability agenda.
  -- Gain knowledge and access appropriate support available for disabled staff.
- Provide the University with the opportunity to:
  -- Consult and collaborate with disabled staff.
  -- Increase disability awareness and push the disability agenda.
  -- Raise the profile of disability within the equality and diversity remit.
  -- Increase accessibility and inclusion across all areas.
  -- Celebrate and promote disability through events and training.
- Share knowledge and ideas.
  -- Increase member knowledge through attendance at external events.
  -- Showcase n-able’s work through social media, web presence and events.
What we have achieved and are working on
The strategic work of n-able feeds into the Equality and Diversity Committee and we work closely alongside the Centre for Inclusion & Diversity (CfID) Team, and other staff networks.
n-able has been consulted on various activities across the University including the Disabled Employee Guide. We contribute to the Equality Impact Assessment analysis and advise on new and updated policies and procedures across the University.
In 2015, n-able achieved "Awesome" network status and the co-chairs received the Inclusive Networks award for Network Leaders of the Year. In 2016, n-able was a key contributor and organiser of the very successful Diversity Festival.
How you can join
Anyone can join us. You do not need to have an impairment or tell us that you are disabled.
You can become a member if you have an interest in disability and would like to contribute to the disability agenda.
We meet in a safe and confidential environment and welcome all opinions and beliefs.
Come and join n-able so that we can continue to have our voices heard!

- Offer mutual support as people explore ways to live out their faith beliefs or philosophies;

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/centre-for-inclusion-and-diversity-intranet/SitePages/Equality-and-Diversity-Structures.aspx

Centre for Inclusion & Diversity (CfID)
Equality, Diversity & Inclusion Structure and Governance
Equality, Diversity & Inclusion Committee (EDIC)
The purpose of the EDIC is to provide an effective strategic guidance and assurance mechanism for Executive Board, People Committee and Council on the delivery of the EDI strategy and the management of EDI related risk. The Committee achieves this through overseeing the framework for implementing and delivering embedded EDI on behalf of the Executive Board.
This Committee is chaired by the Pro-Vice Chancellor Equality, Diversity and of the University and brings together representatives from the wider university.
EDI Delivery Coordination Group (EDCG)
The purpose of the EDCG is a forum for the Associate Deans EDI and Associate Director.
Equality, Diversity & Inclusion Operational Group (EDIOG)
The purpose of the EDIOG is to provide a forum for discussing the priorities and concerns of staff and student equality forums and for addressing wider operational issues relating to EDI at the University. The EDIOG will be responsible for sharing good EDI practices across the university, exploring solutions to operational EDI issues and identifying common themes that point to strategic risks or opportunities and to escalate these to EDIC.
Equality Charters Steering Groups (ECSG)
The purpose of the ECSG is to provide an oversight and co-ordination function on all areas of equality charter mark in respect of students and staff. The ECSG will advise the EDIC on charter mark in relation to gender, race, disability and LGBT+ in respect of students and staff.
Executive EDI Advocate (EBA)
Each equality staff network and area of diversity will have its own Executive Advocate, who is a member of the Exec. Board. Executive Advocates provide communication between the networks and the Exec Board. They act as role models and spokesperson for their diversity area, and for EDI in general.
All EB members are committed to the principles of EDI by taking on the role of Executive Advocates. This includes a joint commitment to eliminating discrimination, advancing equality of opportunity, fostering good relations, and supporting the continued development of positive cultural messages across the entire University community.
Our Executive EDI Advocates will take responsibility for identifying and raising potential diversity and inclusion-related issues in meetings and discussions across the University.
Through this programme, EB members will collectively:
- Acknowledge their role in leading from the front as one of the most effective means of advancing the EDI agenda within the University.
- Understand that their visible support of EDI demonstrates to any possible opponents that they are out of step with the University’s key EDI messages.
- Are firmly committed to considering the possible impact of institutional practices on the diversity areas that they champion by embodying EDI principles in their day-to-day interactions with staff, students and others associated with the University.
- Strongly inspire leaders/managers at all levels to support staff in discharging their EDI responsibilities, network activities and events at the University.
While all EB members will be taking a collaborative and intersectional approach to EDI, specific involvement in EDI activities within protected characteristics /identified diversity areas will undoubtedly vary over time.
Executive Advocates for EDI areas:
1. Disability [Steve Gilley]
2. Race Equality [Andrew Lang]
3. Gender Equality [Paul Watson and Claire Pryke]
4. LGBT+ Equality [Martin Conway]
5. Neuro Diversity [Dave Harris]
6. Religion/Belief [Prof. Shirley Congdon]
7. Age [Prof. Sherif El-Khamisy]
8. Marriage and Civil Partnership [Prof. Martin Priest]
9. Socio-economic equality [Prof. Engobo Emeseh]
10. Wellbeing and Mental Health [Prof. Rob James]
11. Pregnancy, maternity, parents and carers [Dr Anne Miller]
EDI Facilitator
Faculty Staff
An EDI Facilitator will work collaboratively with the Faculty Associate Dean, Equality, Diversity and Inclusion, Heads of School and with the Faculty’s Equality, Diversity and Inclusion Committee on the development and implementation of the EDI agenda within their School and the Faculty.
