Postgraduate Researchers
Support for PGRs
Induction Programmes
The University Induction Programme is delivered three times per academic year, following each new student intake, for new Postgraduate Research (PGR) students. In addition, Faculty induction will take place following each new intake of students.
The Faculty and University induction sessions are a requirement for all new PGR students across the University, whether part-time or full-time.
Induction will help you understand the academic and social environment in which you are working and provide an introduction to a range of University services available to you.
In addition, Faculties/Departments or individual Supervisors provide induction to your specific research area. The arrangements will be provided by your PGR Administrator.
PGR Representatives
PGR Representatives (reps) gather feedback and opinions and speak on matters raised in various committees held across the University. Reps effectively act as the voice for their fellow PGRs with the aim to develop and improve the experience for PGRs at Bradford.
You can feedback to your PGR rep on various issues and they can take any concern you may have to forums where this can be fed back and acted upon by the University.
A list of current PGR reps can be found on the PGR Representatives section of the BRDF SharePoint. It also gives you more information about how you can volunteer as a rep. Volunteering as a rep offers the opportunity to develop new and existing skills, thus enhancing your CV and employability. You will also gain insight into the research governance and strategy in a UK academic environment in Higher Education, which is particularly relevant to those wanting a career in academia. The rep roles map with the national Researcher Development Framework used in Bradford's DNA process, so consider running to be a rep if you want to develop any of these areas:
- B1 Personal qualities - integrity and responsibility
- B3 Professional and career development - continuing professional development and networking
- D1 Working with others - collegiality, team working, influence and leadership
- D3 Engagement and impact - policy, society and culture
Support for Personal and Professional Development
Supervisors will complete a development needs analysis with you to identify any training and development you will require to support your research. There is further provision of skills development which can be accessed across the University.
Teaching or Support teaching
The Bradford:Fellowships scheme offers a coherent, whole career framework to support the development of those involved in teaching and/or the support of learning. There a three intakes per year and these can be found, along with application details on the Bradford:Fellowships website.
Training and Development for Doctoral Students
Please see the Bradford Researcher Development Framework website.
PG Researcher Development
Please see the PG researcher development webpage to access Doctoral Skills appointments and new resources available. Also there is Handy Hints for PhD students which is full of useful information.
Workshops and Clinics
Check out the Workshops and Clinics website for information.
Library workshops for Researchers
The Library offers workshops to all research students - please go to the library webpage for more details. As well as research specific workshops, the library has generic workshops that you may find of interest.
Bradford Researcher Development Framework (BRDF)
The Bradford Researcher Development Framework (BRDF) is a programme that provides a flexible, inclusive and progressive structure to enhance the University's approach to the development and training of PGRs.
You can find upcoming sessions, along with a list of our programme of training below, plus information on the Development Needs Analysis process for PGRs and guidance for supervisors.
No matter which stage of the PGR journey you are at, you can find related guidance on the milestones coming up and development opportunities which are relevant to you.
Facilities available for PGRs
PGR Lounge
Social learning space dedicated exclusively to PGRs at the University of Bradford.
Located in Carlton Building on the city centre campus, it includes group study facilities, bookable meeting spaces, quiet study areas and social spaces.
Other campus facilities
Other facilities available to you will vary from one research area to another for a number of factors, including the nature of the research and the size of the academic area concerned. You should be advised before the start of your programme what facilities you will be provided with. The facilities provided should normally include the following (for certain items it may be necessary for a charge to be made):
- library resources including access to journals, databases and other electronic resources, as well as InterLibrary Loans and SCONUL Access (which allows you to use other university libraries).
- IT resources including word processing, spreadsheet packages, statistical and graphics packages, email and internet access.
- working space with a desk and associated storage, preferably within your immediate work environment.
- where appropriate to the research project, access to laboratory facilities, laboratory consumables, scientific instrumentation, photographic services and software commensurate with the nature and possible scope of the research.
- photocopying facilities and, where directly relevant to the research project, appropriate access to internal and external telephone networks.
Your Faculty/Department is expected to ensure that you are provided with appropriate training to enable you to use such facilities effectively. If it is the policy to charge students for using any of these facilities, in full or in part, this should be clearly stated in the information made available to you by the Faculty/Department prior to registration.
Academic English Language Support
The University Language Centre offer a range of English language classes and support to help you get the most from your studies at the University of Bradford. Please use the link above for more information.
Pastoral care
Any problems arising during the course of a PGRs research should normally be discussed initially with the Principal Supervisor.
If a student feels their work is not proceeding satisfactorily for reasons beyond their control, they may:
- contact their Faculty/Department Director of Postgraduate Research (or an alternative if this person is their own Supervisor).
- discuss a matter with someone outside their Faculty/Department i.e. their Head of Department, Dean of Faculty or the Chair of the Faculty Post Graduate Research Degrees Committee.
PGRs are advised that any problems arising during their period of study should be brought to the attention of the Director of Postgraduate Research (or other persons as noted above) in order that any difficulties may be addressed and resolved as they arise.
Please note: unless any problems arising regarding inadequacy of supervisory or other arrangements during the period of study are raised before submission of the thesis, no subsequent appeal against a decision of the Examiners may be based on a complaint on these grounds, unless there are exceptional reasons for it not having come to light until after the assessment.
Supervisors
Supervisors should appreciate that these procedures may enable a PGR, if the working relationship has not been established, to initiate an amendment to the supervisory arrangements.
Similarly, a Supervisor having difficulties in establishing a working relationship with a PGR should, in the first instance, discuss this with their Department/Faculty Director of Postgraduate Research and/or Associate Dean. This procedure may, likewise, initiate an amendment in the supervisory arrangements.
Personal harassment
If a PGR feels they are being subject to personal harassment including bullying they should seek advice immediately via the Equality and Diversity Team. Any incident of this nature will be regarded extremely seriously by the University.
Equality, Diversity and Inclusion
The Equality and Diversity Team provides a Dignity and Respect advice service.
Counselling Service
Counselling service provides counselling for both enrolled students and staff at the University. There is a drop-in service during term time or bookable appoints.
Director of Postgraduate Research
A Director of Postgraduate Research is nominated in each Faculty/Department. This member of staff is responsible for:
- all administrative matters relating to the acceptance, progress and assessment of research students in the area concerned;
- providing an independent route if a student wishes to seek advice on any difficulty relating to the supervisory process or are in need of pastoral care (where the Director of Postgraduate Research is the students Supervisor then alternative arrangements will be made);
- ensuring that the student is given the opportunity to provide feedback on academic provision and support facilities.
Faculty Postgraduate Research Sub-Committee
The Faculty Postgraduate Research Sub-Committee is usually chaired by an Associate Dean for Research and Knowledge Transfer and is responsible for the policy, direction and quality assurance of graduate research throughout the University.
Terms of Reference
The Faculty Postgraduate Research Sub-Committees make recommendations to the Learning and Teaching Committee on the following business:
1. To advise and report to the Learning and Teaching Committee on matters of academic policy relating to higher degrees by research
2. To formulate, for consideration by the Learning and Teaching Committee, Ordinances and Regulations for higher degrees by research
3. To make recommendations to the Learning and Teaching Committee arising from periodic, major reviews of academic policy relating to higher degrees by research
4. To formulate, for consideration by the Learning and Teaching Committee, procedures, guidelines and Codes of Practice relating to higher degrees by research
5. To co-ordinate institutional quality assurance and learning support activities, with a view to assuring the security, quality and standards of University awards for higher degrees by research, both in relation to internal and external candidates
6. To inform and consult as appropriate Research Strategy Committee
7. To consider on behalf of the Faculty matters relating to individual candidates for higher degrees by research including the following and, where appropriate, to make recommendations to Senate:
  a. admission
  b. suspension of registration
  c. extension of registration
  d. titles and sub-titles of theses
  e. restriction of access to theses
  f. appointment of examiners
  g. examiners' reports
  h. recommendations for termination of registration
  i. and other matters as may be appropriate.
Breaches, Appeals and Complaints
Find out more on the Academic Misconduct, Appeals and Complaints website.
PGR Connect
PGR Connect is a PGR-led peer support scheme that aims to offer a support network for our postgraduate researchers. The scheme strives to foster a sense of belonging and community, help PGRs adjust to or progress in their studies, and mitigate the sense of isolation that can sometimes develop due to the unique way postgraduate researchers study and work.
For further information please see the PGR Connect site.

Peer Mentoring
Peer Mentoring for Students
Peer mentoring at Bradford aims to support:
1. Student transition into/through university
2. The employability of students that mentor
3. A sense of community & belonging
Peer mentors aid newer students by:
- Offering one-to-one guidance to assigned mentees, or group based support
- Focusing on helping them to adjust to HE life and study, or with a particular area of study
- Empowering them to find their own solutions and build learner independence and confidence
Benefits for stu dent mentees can include:
- Feel less vulnerable or uncertain
- Obtain greater social and academic confidence
- Become aware of other student services and support
- Be more pro-active in their development and learning
Benefits for peer mentors can include:
- Skills, experiences and concrete examples for CVs, applications etc.
- Transferable skills and personal development
- Opportunity to help others
-  Personal satisfaction of making new students feel more welcome
- Being part of/creating a new community
- Enjoyable and rewarding experience – the chance to make a difference
- Peer Mentor role is a Bradford Employability Award for the HEAR
Student/recent graduates apply to be a peer mentor and, if successful, are invited to peer mentor training. Once in the role they receive ongoing support and guidance.
For interest in being a peer mentor please contact the team.

SOURCE_URL: http://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/student-casework-intranet/SitePages/Appeals.aspx

Student Casework
Appeals
About appeals
If a student is not happy they can appeal against a decision made at all stages of the both the Student Fitness to Practice Procedure and Student Disciplinary Procedure.
- For appeals against a Stage One decision: if a student is not satisfied with the outcome of the case at Stage One, they may request that the case be considered at Stage Two of the Procedure.
- For appeals against a Stage Two decision: if the student is not satisfied with the outcome of the case at Stage Two, they may request that the case be considered at Stage Three of the procedure.
- For appeals against a Stage Three decision: if the student is not satisfied with the outcome of the case at Stage Three, they may request that the case be considered at the Appeal Stage of the procedure.
Grounds
Appeals must be submitted to the University Secretary within ten working days of the notification of the initial decision to the student.
An appeal may only be on three grounds:
- Lack of procedural fairness
- New evidence or evidence that was not available at the time of the hearing
- The decision was manifestly unreasonable
The University Secretary will review the appeal against these grounds and determined whether there is sufficient justification to progress the case further.
How to make an appeal
Appeals should be submitted to the University Secretary via governance@bradford,.ac.uk
In all cases, the onus is on the student to demonstrate, through evidence, that they have a valid ground of appeal.
All evidence must be presented with the appeal submission.
Further evidence cannot be presented at an Appeal Panel, although the University Secretary may ask a student to submit further information or evidence to substantiate their appeal.
Once an appeal request has been lodged and accepted, no penalty or sanction will take effect until the appeals procedure has been exhausted but where a student has been suspended from their studies, from accessing services on campus or has been removed from placement, the suspension will remain in place.
Appeal panels
Where a valid appeal against a Stage Three decision has been submitted, a new Panel will review the Appeal as set out in the Appeal Stage of the Process as set out in the Procedure.
The burden of proof at the Appeal Stage is on the student to demonstrate their grounds of appeal.
The Chair of the Appeal Panel will consider whether a hearing is required or if the evidence submitted can be considered without a hearing: if a hearing is required, appeal panel will hear evidence from the student, the investigator and, where appropriate, the Chair of the Stage Three Fitness to Practise Panel.
After considering all the evidence, the panel shall decide whether to reject or uphold the appeal. The student will be informed in writing within ten working days.
The decision of the appeal panel is final and represents a completion of procedure: a Completion of Procedures letter shall be issued.

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/chaplaincy-services-intranet/SitePages/understanding-other-faiths.aspx

haplaincy Services
Understanding Different Faiths
Every religion has its own practices and traditions. Being at university you will meet people who dress in particular ways, eat foods prepared according to certain rules and worship and pray at specific times of day in special places.
Living and working with people who have differences to your own lifestyle is a wonderful opportunity to learn more about the world in which we live. The Chaplaincy is committed to supporting this process and you will see us working with each other through diversity events, celebrating and raising awareness of the different faiths that make up our university community.
University is a safe place for asking questions and experiencing new things, there is always much to learn about each other.
The first three major religions, Islam, Christianity and Judaism are called the Abrahamic religions because they all believe that there is one God who created Heaven and earth. They all have the story of Adam and Eve and their descendants until the time of Abraham, and they all developed in the same part of the world – the Middle East. It is the primary figure in their tradition that led to 3 distinct religions: Judaism follows the Old Testament teaching of Moses primarily; Christians follow the teachings of Jesus Christ in the New Testament while Muslims follow the teaching of the prophet Muhammad (PBUH) in the Qur’an. The root is the same, the branches have evolved differently.
Other religions such as Hinduism, Sikhism and Buddhism, which grew out of the experiences and traditions of Asia. Buddhism isn’t in fact a religion, it is a philosophy. Hinduism celebrates the many facets of the divine as characters through the Vedas: Hymns to the ancient gods. Sikhism follows the teachings of ten Gurus through history who each gave insights into God and encouraged a righteous life. These other religions are very different to each other but also have similarities, for example Hindus believe that Buddha was an incarnation of their God Vishnu. Hindus and Sikhs believe in reincarnation of the soul and the cycle of life and death leading to a higher state and a closer relationship to God. Here in Bradford we have the opportunity to visit many places of worship on the first Saturday of each month from March to October. For further details of the Bradford Faith Trail.
What follows is an attempt to capture an overview of many of the different Religions found within our community. For convenience a framework has been established so that it is convenient to compare different human experiences and life stages. Of necessity this has meant that it is an overview and not a complete guide to every faith and belief practice.
- Baha'i
- Buddhism
- Chinese Community
- Christianity
- Free Churches or Non-Conformsist/Protestant
- Othodox Churches, - Eastern, Greek & Russian
- Quakers
- Humanism
- Hinduism
- Islam
- Judaism
- Sikhism
- Travellers
Spiritual Care for People of Different Faiths
Introduction
This is an attempt to support anyone who is involved in caring for people with a range of spiritual, religious, or cultural needs. It is not designed to be the last word in these things, but rather a general introduction that will hopefully encourage you to begin confident conversations with the people for whom you care. No single person comes as a textbook case. Everyone has their own personal set of traditions, cultures, and expectations. To understand these better, you should have open conversations where you can share, learn, and appreciate the different views of other people.
For balance and ease this information provides a set layout to provide a comparative approach to key life events in each of the main faith communities, here in Bradford.
What do we mean by “spiritual needs?
People may not fully understand our language, when we talk about “Spirituality”, but every single person has a right and a need to have their uniqueness recognised, appreciated, and valued. Every time that we experience emotions such as awe and wonder, grief and loss, joy, and contentment, we are responding to something “other”, something external to who we are. This booklet is a tool to help people who care for others, to recognise the significance of those responses, and to understand the place that faith and belief might have in influencing decisions that your clients might make.
One definition of spirituality is “the search for meaning and purpose in life”. Religion and Religious practice/observance may be one way of exercising that search. A conversation with someone about their spiritual needs, which may uncover an existing faith practice could be framed around the following questions:
1. Do you have a belief that draws on a specific religious tradition? Is this better understood as a philosophy of life drawn from an organisation?
2. Tell me about some of your beliefs and truths, that are part of your tradition.
3. How do these beliefs influence your view of life and your daily practice?
4. Are there specific rituals or expressions of faith that you are required to practice daily?
5. Are there artifacts or rituals or possessions which bring spiritual comfort or support?
These questions are not specific to any one tradition or philosophy. The answers offered may or may not give you clues about the beliefs that a person has but will open the conversation for further discussion. People with a faith, belief or philosophy often appreciate being asked about this, so don’t be afraid to seek clarification about anything that you don’t understand. The important thing is that you do not make assumptions about people’s beliefs and behaviours. Not every follower of a particular belief behaves, responds, or acts in the same way to another. A good example would be the assumption that someone would not eat a certain food because it is often identified as being prohibited by a religious group.
The key to real understanding is the appreciation of the uniqueness and individuality of the person in front of you.
By talking openly and confidently you will learn much that will influence the kind of care that you can offer, the relationship that you have with that person, and ultimately your own personal knowledge and professional practice.
Under the Equality and Human Rights Act of 2010, it is against the law to discriminate against someone because of their protected characteristic. These include:
- Age
- Disability
- Gender reassignment
- Marriage and civil partnership
- Pregnancy and maternity
- Race
- Religion or belief
- Sex (gender)
- Sexual orientation.
Religion refers to any religion, including the right to have no religion. Belief refers to any religious or philosophical belief and includes the right to have no prescribed belief system. Generally, a belief system influences your life choices or the way that you live your life for it to be included in this definition.
People do not fit into neat categories and so it is also important to consider issues around intersectionality when supporting an individual. This means that you may find yourself reflecting on what life is like for a disabled atheist transwoman, a divorced Polish Roman Catholic, or a young gay Muslim man? These are just examples obviously, but very few people are defined by one single characteristic, and the expectations of one characteristic may well cause areas of friction within another.

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/:u:/r/sites/chaplaincy-services-intranet/SitePages/HUMANISM.aspx

Chaplaincy Services
HUMANISM
Throughout recorded history there have been many non-religious people who have believed that this life is the only life we have, that the universe is a natural phenomenon with no supernatural side, and that we can live ethical and fulfilling lives based on reason and humanity. Humanists have trusted to the scientific method, evidence, and reason to discover truths about the universe and have placed human welfare and happiness at the centre of their ethical decision making.
Many millions of people in the UK share this way of living and of looking at the world, but many of them may not have heard the word ‘humanist’ and perhaps don’t realise that it describes what they believe.
Leadership:
Humanist UK (www.humanist.uk) advance free thinking and promote humanism to create a tolerant society where rational thinking and kindness prevail.
Diet:
Many humanists have become vegetarian or vegan, but one must not generalise.
Death:
No particular rites.
Last Offices:
Usual procedures.
Autopsy/Transplants:
No objections
Funeral:
Naming ceremonies, marriage and funeral ceremonies can all be arranged with a humanist celebrant to assist. Non-religious ceremonies are shaped around the person involved and often feature stories about the person. For a Humanist these ceremonies represent something essential and timeless – the shared values and narratives that connect us with each other,
Obviously, no Sacred Texts:
Many books are available now about humanism, and there are significant writers who have identified themselves as such, David Hume, Charles Darwin, Thomas Hardy, Albert Einstein, George Orwell, Marie Curie, and Richard Dawkins.
Frequently Asked Questions
What is Humanism - is it a religion?
Humanism is explicitly not a religion, but it is considered a worldview. Roughly speaking, the word humanist has come to mean someone who:
- trusts to the scientific method when it comes to understanding how the universe works, and rejects the idea of the supernatural (and is therefore an atheist or agnostic).
- makes their ethical decisions based on reason, empathy, and a concern for human beings and other sentient animals.
- believes that, in the absence of an afterlife and any discernible purpose to the universe, human beings can act to give their own lives meaning by seeking happiness in this life and helping others to do the same.
Oh, so humanists are just angry atheists then who are anti-religious?
Not at all. Humanism is a positive non-religious worldview that is tolerant of a range of viewpoints and outlooks. Most humanists don't mind what others believe and usually share many of the same values of tolerance and compassion for others that is seen in the 'golden rule' idea throughout human belief. Humanists dislike discrimination, though so they do sometimes campaign to challenge perceived religious privilege such as with faith schools, Bishops in the House of Lords, or laws on blasphemy. Of course like any group or outlook there are a range of opinions, including some who are more hostile to religious belief.
Are there many humanists? How do I know if I am one?
Humanism is not new. With the British Social Attitudes Survey showing that as much as 53% of the UK population is non-religious, further analysis points to about half of these people sharing humanist outlooks. Numbers are far higher among younger age groups too. Although the term is not commonly used it is increasing, and the philosophy behind humanism goes back to Ancient Greece, the renaissance, and thinkers from the age of reason and beyond. If you'd like to learn more why not take the quiz to find out if you might be a humanist?

When to contact a Chaplain
The University of Bradford Chaplaincy Service is here to support everyone, student, staff, family and friends.
Chaplains at the University Chaplaincy here in Bradford can support you or accompany you on occasions such as:
- you’re finding university life difficult and need someone to talk to;
- you need time to think; you need help in times of crisis;
- you need to make a decision and need support in doing so;
- you regret what has happened and need to move on;
- someone has died and you need support;
- you want to deepen your spiritual experience;
- you have personal struggles or relationship problems.
Chaplains have links with specialist sources of help on campus here in Bradford and part of Student Life and Wellbeing Services. We can help you to access appropriate support.
The Chaplaincy can also signpost you to different places of worship for each faith across the City of Bradford.
The Co-ordinating Chaplain can put students and staff in touch with other faith traditions, the best way to make contact is via email to chaplain@bradford.ac.uk, or by telephone to 07901 670365.

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/:u:/r/sites/chaplaincy-services-intranet/SitePages/TRAVELLERS.aspx

Chaplaincy Services
TRAVELLERS
Belief:
Essentially Christian, mostly either Irish or European Roman Catholic.
Diet:
No special requirements but make a cross-reference the Roman Catholic notes about fasting for holy days.
Death:
Again, follow Roman Catholic guidance.
Autopsy/ Transplants:
No objections to transplants and transfusions.
Funeral:
Big family and society gatherings, following Roman Catholic Mass, opportunity to celebrate wealth and success of the bereaved.
Sacred Books:
The Bible.
Special notes:
The Irish Traveller community has a long history, with its own traditions, language, practical skills, culture, arts and music, with distinctive patterns of living (e.g. nomadism,) keeping of horses, and strong values built around families, care and extended families. One of the biggest influences on Traveller culture is the nomadic lifestyle, which has differentiated between Traveller and “settled” lifestyles. Nomadic lifestyles serve three functions within the community; they are social, economic, and cultural.
The situation of Travellers is a difficult one, with the traditional way of life. These difficulties are characterised by the following:-
- Poor living conditions; many in unauthorized sites and finding it difficult to obtain either sites or accommodation of reasonable quality in acceptable locations.
- Ill-health; evidenced in life expectancies which remain typically ten years less than the settled population.
- Limited educational achievement; a reflection of the lack of opportunities, with few Travellers progressing to second level (less than 3% completing) and only a handful individuals reaching third level.
- Discrimination; Travellers constitute the largest single category of discrimination reported to the responsible agencies.
- Identity Issues; problems of identity arising from their minority status.

--- SOURCE SEPARATOR ---

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/:u:/r/sites/chaplaincy-services-intranet/SitePages/BAHA%27I.aspx

Chaplaincy Services
BAHA'I
Baha'is believe that God periodically reveals his will through divine messengers, whose purpose is to transform the character of humankind and to develop, within those who respond, moral and spiritual qualities. Their Religion is seen as orderly, unified, and progressive from age to age. Bahāʾīs believe in the oneness of humanity and devote themselves to the abolition of racial, class, and religious prejudices. The great bulk of Bahāʾī teachings is concerned with social ethics. The faith has no priesthood and does not observe ritual forms in its worship. Founded in 1844 in Iran, it is the youngest of the world’s religions.
Leadership
The Baha’i Faith have no clergy. Leadership belongs within the community and its administrative order is based on the election of representatives from within the community of the believers by secret ballot.
Sacraments
There are no rituals or sacraments used within worship. There is the recital of a daily obligatory prayer, which is performed individually in private and at a time and place of their choosing. I bear witness, O my God, that Thou hast created me to know Thee and to worship Thee. I testify, at this moment, to my powerlessness and to Thy might, to my poverty and to Thy wealth. There is none other God but Thee.
Diet
There are no clear dietary restrictions, however alcohol and addictive drugs are forbidden unless advised and prescribed by a physician.

--- SOURCE SEPARATOR ---

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/:u:/r/sites/chaplaincy-services-intranet/SitePages/ORTHODOX-CHURCHES,---EASTERN---GREEK-%26-RUSSIAN.aspx

Chaplaincy Services
ORTHODOX CHURCHES, - EASTERN / GREEK & RUSSIAN
The origins of this group of people are found in the Eastern Orthodox Churches which are mainly spread throughout the middle East, Russia, and Egypt (where they are sometimes called Coptic Churches). As migration spread across Europe and into the United Kingdom a few of these Churches became established. In Bradford there is St Joseph’s Orthodox, and a Serbian Orthodox Church both within Little Horton. They are part of the oldest form of the Christian Church and split from the Roman Catholics in the year 1054.
They are very similar in their belief and practice to other Christian Churches, their main disagreements were over the use of leavened or unleavened bread, the role of the Pope in Roman and the See (head) in Constantinople.
Leadership:
Archbishops, Bishops, and Priests, usually known as Father.
Sacraments:
As Roman Catholic
Diet:
Lent Observance (6 weeks up to Easter) taken most seriously.
Death:
May request anointing with oil.
Last Offices:
Usual procedures
Autopsy/Transplants:
No objections on Faith grounds.
Funeral:
Either Burial or Cremation.
Sacred Book:
Bible
NB. Most Orthodox Churches use the Julian Calendar so most Festivals will be celebrated approx. 13 days after Churches using a western Calendar.

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/:u:/r/sites/chaplaincy-services-intranet/SitePages/Chinese-Community.aspx

Chaplaincy Services
Chinese Community
Within the United Kingdom the Chinese Community maintain a significant but almost invisible presence. Often families may be living in isolation, which means that close knit family relationships are very important. This cultural community traditionally follow one of four major religions, though each of the four may have their own distinct Chinese influence: The four are:
- Confucianism
- Taoism
- Buddhism
- Roman Catholic Christianity.
Whist the Buddhist and Roman Catholic traditions are described in more detail elsewhere in this booklet, it is worth exploring the other two.
Confucianism:
In China this is also known as Juchiaco, the sect of the Jus, which is a moral code of conduct. Confucianism believes in ancestor worship and human-centred virtues for living a peaceful life. The golden rule of Confucianism is “Do not do unto others what you would not want others to do unto you.” There is still a debate between scholars as to whether Confucianism constitutes a religion. Confucianism is a way of life propagated by Confucius in the 6th–5th century BCE and followed by the Chinese people for more than two millennia. It remains the social code of many Chinese and continues to influence other countries, particularly Korea, Japan and Vietnam. Confucian philosophy states that “Fate” controls worldly events, and the Divine will regulate the order of nature. There are no Confucian gods, and Confucius himself is worshipped as a spirit rather than a god. However, there are temples of Confucianism, which are places where important community and civic rituals happen.
Taoism:
Taoism has been connected to the philosopher Lao Tzu, who around 500 BCE wrote the main book of Taoism, the Tao Te Ching. Taoism holds that humans and animals should live in balance with the Tao, or the universe. Taoists believe in spiritual immortality, where the spirit of the body joins the universe after death. Taoism is more mystical and less pragmatic than Confucianism. The Tao focuses on ideals of balance and order, and often uses nature as a metaphor. Taoism, unlike Confucianism, rejects rank and class. Taoists shun aggression, competition, and ambition, and promote obedience, respect, and kindness.
Taoists stress the importance of harmonizing with nature by balancing the yin and yang and developing chi through meditation and disengagement. The human body is regarded as a source of chi-derived energy, which some people have the power to concentrate and congeal into an essence. Chi (also spelled ch'i or qi) is variously known as the "breath of heaven," “mystical breath," the "breath of nature" and the "quality of spirit"
In classic Taoist belief, matter and energy are thought to be governed by five basic movements. The strength and influence of these movements wax and wane over the course of a year; with wood peaking during spring, fire during summer, metal in autumn and water in winter. The remaining movement, earth, asserts its presence most powerfully during the periods before the start of each season.

--- SOURCE SEPARATOR ---

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/:u:/r/sites/chaplaincy-services-intranet/SitePages/Christianity.aspx

Chaplaincy Services
CHRISTIANITY
At its most basic, Christianity is the faith tradition that focuses on the figure of Jesus Christ. In this context, faith refers both to the believers' act of trust and to the content of their faith. As a tradition, Christianity is more than a system of religious belief. Christians believe that there is only one God, whom they call Father, as Jesus Christ taught them to do. Jesus is recognised as the true son of God and functions via the Trinity.
Christians recognise Jesus as the Son of God who was sent to save humanity from death and sin. Jesus Christ taught that he was the Son of God. His teachings can be summarised, briefly as the love of God and love of one's neighbour. Jesus said that he had come to fulfil God's laws and the prophecies which were taught in the Old Testament, to demonstrate how to live it rather than teach it.
A major Christian doctrine is the Justification by faith: Christians believe that through their belief in Jesus and in his death and resurrection, they can have a close relationship with God, whose forgiveness was made once and for all through the death of Jesus Christ.
Christians believe in the Trinity - that is, in God as Father, Son and Holy Spirit. Some confuse this and think that Christians believe in three separate gods, which they don't. Christians believe that God took on the human form in Jesus Christ, and that God is present today through the work of the Holy Spirit, which is evident in the actions of believers. Christians believe in a life after death. Christians believe that many spiritual experiences in this life help to give them some idea of what eternal life will be like.
Over the centuries The Church as the earthly body of Christ has been devolved and schisms have created a multitude of denominations. The three main ones are described below.
Roman Catholic Church
The Roman Catholic Church traces its history right back to Jesus Christ and the Apostles. The apostle Peter was made earthly head of the Church, prior to the Ascension of Jesus Christ. It is assumed that he was then the first Pope and each one since can be traced back to Peter via spiritual succession. Over the course of the centuries, the RC Church developed a highly sophisticated theology and an elaborate organizational structure headed by the papacy, the oldest continuing absolute monarchy in the world. Through mission and movement there has been a worldwide spread of this Church with active ministry especially in South America, Europe, the Far East and Africa.
Leadership:
Each provincial area is managed by a Cardinal, who then appoints bishops, priests, and deacons. Lay ministry is recognised, but the ordination of women to the Priesthood is not yet accepted. Women have traditionally been called into a life of mission prayer and service through becoming a nun or sister.
Sacraments:
Baptism, Holy Communion (Known as Mass) Marriage, Ordination, Confirmation, Confession, and anointing with oil (Unction).
Diet:
Some traditionally may prefer to not eat meat on a Friday and to remember the crucifixion. The period of preparation for Easter known as Lent is traditionally a fasting time, having followed Shrove or Pancake Tuesday, when traditionally all perishable foods will have been consumed.
Death:
Holy Communion, the sacrament of the sick, and the anointing of the person will be offered to the dying by a Roman Catholic Priest. Last Rites can be administered up to two hours after the death.
Last Offices:
See above about Last Rites.
Autopsy/Transplant:
No religious objections
Funeral:
Usually Burial with a requiem Mass held afterwards.
Sacred Book:
The Holy Bible
Anglican or Church of England:
Leadership:
Following a spit with the Roman Catholic Church, King Henry VIII declared himself the sovereign head of the Church of England. This as been passed on through the royal dynasties. The monarch appoints two Archbishops (Canterbury and York) to lead the two provinces, which in turn are managed by Bishops, and Clergy. Local clergy are often called by various titles: Rector, Vicar, Curate, Reverend. The Church of England recognise both men and women through ordination, and there is also a very active use of lay licensed ministry. Staff connected to Cathedrals are often called canons and may be lay or ordained.
Sacraments:
There are only two sacraments, Baptism, and the Eucharist, but the Church of England then honours confirmation, ordination, marriage, reconciliation of the penitent, and the anointing of the sick as important religious rites.
Diet:
As in the Roman Catholic Church.
Death:
Emergency baptism may be requested for the dying if they are known to have not been baptised, especially newly born infants. Anyone may administer emergency baptism “in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit, Amen”. There are also set Last Rites, and a communion service(or mass) for the dying. Families may request anointing with oil, for the person nearing the end of their life. Prayers with the family afterwards are also requested.
Last Office:
Usual nursing procedures.
Autopsy/transplant:
No religious objections
Funeral:
Traditionally burial, but more and more families are accepting cremation as the standard procedure.
Sacred Book:
The Bible, consisting of the Old and the New Testaments.
Frequently Asked Questions
Why are there so many different Christian denominations and societies?
There are today a broad range of expressions of Christian belief, from the very informal small community groups to the more formal and structured bodies such as the Church of England or Roman Catholicism. Within the University there is a range of different Societies catering to a range of different people within the Christian tradition. To find out more about what each society offers, go along and get a feel for what it’s like, or message them and ask what their main emphasis is. To find out about the different denominations you can talk to Andrew and he will be more than happy to answer any questions you might have about the denominations within Christianity. Some of the reasons for this are historical, some theological, some stylistic, and usually it’s a combination of all of those that leads people towards one denomination or another.
What is Holy Communion?
Whatever denomination, Christians all believe that Jesus Christ was the son of God; that he died on the cross and then rose from the grave. In dying without having committed a crime he has paid the price for all of our sins. We remember this through the Holy Communion. Often celebrated once a week, Christians gather to share bread and wine or grape juice that has been blessed by a minister. We use the words that Jesus said at the Last Supper with his disciples. The bread represents his body which was broken for us. The wine represents His blood which was shed for us. We pray and acknowledge that we have done and thought wrong things, we ask for forgiveness, and then each take a little piece of the bread and wine. In many churches, anyone can join in the communion service. In some churches it is expected that you will have been confirmed as a Christian before taking the bread and wine. Check with the minister if you are not sure.

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/:u:/r/sites/chaplaincy-services-intranet/SitePages/BUDDHISM.aspx

Chaplaincy Services
BUDDHISM
Buddhism is one of the world's largest religions and originated 2,500 years ago in India. Buddhists believe that the human life is one of suffering, and that meditation, spiritual and physical labour, and good behaviour are the ways to achieve enlightenment, or nirvana. It was brought to the Western world from India, China, Japan and Nepal, and has developed into a worldwide philosophy with different schools represented in many different countries. Some of these schools are more humanistic methods of philosophy, whilst others have elements of a more spiritual religion. The main framework is based upon the teachings of the Buddha (which translated from Sanskrit means the “Awakened One”), who was a teacher who lived in northern India between the mid-6th and mid-4th centuries BCE (before the Common Era). There is no belief in a deity as such, reward or punishment comes through the next life as a consequence of your actions. Enlightenment is the goal of life.
Leadership
Local schools of practice are led by local community leaders. Groups for Meditation are often advertised through educational channels and Adult education providers.
Diet:
Many Buddhists are vegetarian or vegan as it strikes a balance with their desire to do no harm, however this should never be assumed.
Death:
Distress may be caused by offering certain palliative drugs and medication. Especially if there is a sedative which may reduce consciousness. This is because the state of mind at death is of great importance. Often chanting and meditation may be offered. The state of mind influences the character of the rebirth, and peace (enlightenment). This is the ultimate aim. Buddhists seek detailed information regarding their death, to prepare themselves or their loved ones. Open honest and frank discussions are appreciated, giving as accurate a prognosis as possible about the predicted time of death and the symptoms exhibited. Death is a positive transition and therefore they will prefer to be as clear minded as possible. Some Schools of Buddhism will have monks or priests who would be willing to chant prayers for the deceased.
Last offices:
There are no specific Buddhist requirements. It is always useful to consult with family members.
Autopsy/Transplant:
There are no religious objections to post-mortems.
Funeral
Cremation is usual followed by the burial of the ashes. The preferred time frame between the death and the funeral is from three to seven days.
Sacred Books:
Known as the Tripitaka, the sacred texts of Buddhism are broken down into three sections known as baskets. Referred to in the West as the Three Baskets, the Tripitaka includes the Vinaya Pitaka, the Sutta Pitaka, and the Abhidhamma Pitaka.
Festivals:
There are two main festivals in the Buddhist calendar. These are Wesak (or Vesak, is also known as Buddha Day and celebrates the Buddha’s enlightenment.) and Parinirvana Day (a day is to recollect the Buddha’s life and death. Buddhists think about their lives and where they are in relationship to the state of enlightenment or nirvana.) In addition, Buddhists may also take part in retreats, which allow for spiritual development.

--- SOURCE SEPARATOR ---

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/:u:/r/sites/chaplaincy-services-intranet/SitePages/QUAKERS.aspx

Chaplaincy Services
QUAKERS
The Quakers were formed by George Fox, no ordained ministry, all equal, worship often held in silence as followers await a message or mental picture to share with the group. Focus on human Rights, Peace and anti-war campaigning.
Frequently Asked Questions
Are Quakers Christian?
The Religious Society of Friends (Quakers) was formed in England during the turbulent and dynamic 17th century by George Fox. Quakerism is rooted in Christianity and Quakers are encouraged to learn from the life and teaching of Jesus as well as from a wide variety of other texts. However, many modern day Quakers, especially in Britain, find a home in the Society even if they do not identify as Christian. The essentials of Quakerism, which we call our Testimonies, can be embraced by people across a broad spectrum of belief.
How do Quakers worship?
We gather in silent waiting in our Meetings for Worship. If someone feels moved to speak (we call this 'ministering') during the Meeting, they do so. Some Meetings might be totally silent but often there is quite a lot of ministry. Our silence is a means of deepening our shared stillness.
Are Quakers pacifist?
The testimony most people associate with Quakers is our Peace Testimony. We believe that war and preparations for war are contrary to the teachings of Jesus and the will of God.

--- SOURCE SEPARATOR ---

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/:u:/r/sites/chaplaincy-services-intranet/SitePages/SIKHISM.aspx

Chaplaincy Services
SIKHISM
The word Sikh literally means discipline, disciple of follower of Sikh Gurus. Sikhism is a monotheistic religion with a belief in only one God. It emphasises social and sexual equality and stresses the importance of doing good deeds as compared to simply performing “rituals”.
The Sikh religion was founded in the18 century CE by Guru Nanak in the Punjab region that now divides India and Pakistan. The religion is based on the teachings of Guru Nanak and the 9 other Sikh Gurus who followed him. Sikhism is Britain’s fourth most popular religion with just over half a million followers. The number of Sikhs all over the world in estimated at 30 million.
Leaders:
Every gurdwara includes a commercial kitchen as well as a hall for community worship and meals (langar hall). Sikhism has no clergy. Anyone qualified may fill any role in worship. A scholar of Sikhism (gyani) leads the congregation in prayer, although they are considered religious officials in Sikhism, they are not considered to be the equivalent of a priest, as the belief is that there are no such religious intermediaries. The Sikh believes that a human being is a combination of body and soul.
Names:
Sikh names are distinct, and follow a formula: Male -Singh, Female -Kaur is included in the family names for example: Davinder Kaur Bhuller, and Sanjit Singh Bhuller.
Diet:
No Beef in any form, some may not eat Pork, No alcohol or tobacco.
Washing:
As with most people from an Asian background, free flowing water is usually preferred.
Death:
Death may be regarded as a step in life and not necessarily an occasion for mourning. A devout Sikh may wish to recite hymns from the Guru Granth Sahib or hear family members do so. Many families may wish to wash and lay out the body themselves. The five symbols of Sikhism, worn by most Sikh men should not be removed, the hair and beard should be left uncut, and the turban left in place. The body should be straightened, and wearing the Kaccha, they should be wrapped in a plain white sheet. Sikhs will usually be cremated, preferably within 24 hours of death.
The five “K’s”
- Kesh: uncut hair and beard, men keep their hair under a turban.
- Kanga: a small comb always worn in the hair.
- Kara: A steel bangle worn on the right wrist.
- Kirpan: A short sword or dagger.
- Kaccha: A special type of knee length underwear.
Autopsy/ Transplants:
Sikhs do not like the idea of a post mortem, but will obviously accept it if it is legally unavoidable.
Funeral:
Stillborn and neonates are buried, otherwise, if possible, cremation within 24 hours.
Sacred Books:
Guru Granth Sahib, wrapped in a costly cloth.
Temple:
The Gurdwara
Special Notes:
The Sikh believes that a human being is a combination of body and soul. Not only does a Sikh have to remain under physical discipline, but has to follow certain moral laws as well. A human has to perform three duties, to oneself, to society, and to the creator. Those who understand the duties to yourself and the wider society are often held in high regard.
A Sikh aims to live honestly, to treat everyone equally, and to be generous to those in need. To work hard and serve others, to rise early and perform prayers, to abstain from alcohol tobacco or recreational drugs.

--- SOURCE SEPARATOR ---

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/:u:/r/sites/chaplaincy-services-intranet/SitePages/FREE-CHURCHES-or-NON-CONFORMIST-PROTESTANT.aspx

Chaplaincy Services
FREE CHURCHES or NON-CONFORMIST/PROTESTANT
Over the Centuries the Christian Church has experienced division and separatism usually over matters of doctrine or management. There are many different Christian Denominations that are part of the Free Church/Protestant traditions. Most believe the same basic creed that Jesus was both human and divine, that his sacrificial death bought forgiveness. They also believe in the Trinity of Father Son and Holy Spirit.
Leadership:
Some Churches still call and recognise Clergy to be ordained, others appoint leaders from within heir community. Leaders are known as Ministers, Pastors, Deacons, or Elders.
Diet:
No restrictions on religious grounds.
Death
Prayers may be said for the dying person and their family/friends.
Last Offices:
Usual procedures but always useful to talk to the family.
Autopsy/Transplants:
No objections on religious grounds.
Funeral:
May be burial or cremation
Sacred Book
The Holy Bible.
Within this category may be found:
The Methodist Church, founded by John and Charles Wesley (both Anglican Priests) Strong tradition of regular small groups, and hymn singing.
The United Reformed Church. Founded by bringing together the Congregational and Presbyterian Church. There are still independent Congregational and Presbyterian Churches scattered around the United Kingdom.
The Baptist Church. With a history of schism and split there are a variety of Baptist Churches from the Baptist Union, through to the Gospel Standard Strict and Particular Baptists. They all reject infant baptism and reserve it for adults who have confessed their faith, repented of their sins, and sought Baptism by total immersion.
The Church of Scotland: akin to the Church of England, but with Presbyterian roots and The Episcopal Church of Scotland akin to the Roman Catholic.
Pentecostal: Focus on the blessing of the Holy Spirit, praying and speaking in other tongues, seeking prophecies and healing.
The Salvation Army: Not sacramental, they are styled on a military structure, focus on social action with the poor, traditionally nondrinking or use of addictive drugs including tobacco.
The Quakers: Formed by George Fox, no ordained ministry, all equal, worship often held in silence as followers await a message or mental picture to share with the group. Focus on human Rights, Peace and anti-war campaigning.
The Brethren: No hierarchy, recognise different ministry in different individuals.
Many African Free Churches have recently become established and also More Free Charismatic Evangelical Churches, with links to “New Wine” or American movements.
In addition to the above there are several other groups who do not fit with the above but still generally have their roots within the Christian tradition.
These would include the Church of Jesus Christ and Latter-Day Saints (Mormons), The Jehovahs Witnesses, Christian Scientists, Seventh Day Adventists, Plymouth Brethren and many more.

--- SOURCE SEPARATOR ---

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/:u:/r/sites/chaplaincy-services-intranet/SitePages/HINDUISM.aspx

Chaplaincy Services
HINDUISM
Hinduism is the world's oldest religion, according to many scholars, with roots and customs dating back more than 4,000 years. Today, with about 900 million followers, Hinduism is the third-largest religion behind Christianity and Islam. Almost 95 percent of the world's Hindus live in India. Hindus believe in one God and worship that one God under many different manifestations, deities, or images.
Examples of Hindu deities are Krishna, Shiva, Rama, and Durga. Hindus believe that existence is a cycle of birth, death, and rebirth, governed by karma which is a complex belief in cause and effect. You get in the next life what you deserve from the last.
Leadership:
In a local Temple or Cultural Centre there is usually a Priest taking care of the worship space.
Diet:
Many Hindus are vegetarian, they will not eat animal products, beef, ham, bacon, pork. They will not eat off a plate where meat has been previously served.
Washing:
Free flowing water is ideal, so a container of water is helpful for example if a person is in hospital and requires a bedpan or WC. Hygiene rules are very important. Modesty is important. A female Hindu will prefer to be treated by a woman care professional.
Death:
Where possible Hindus would prefer to die at home, they may wish to lie on the floor closer to the earth. A Priest may perform rituals involving tying a thread around the persons neck or wrist, sprinkling them with Holy water from the Ganges River, placing a sacred leaf in the person’s mouth. The body should not be washed without first informing the family, so communication is vital. Grief is often expressed vocally with great weeping and wailing. Traditionally the eldest son will take part in the washing and preparing of the body, and it is preferable that non-Hindus do not touch the body. After death the body should not be left uncovered, and jewellery and the sacred threads should not be removed.
Last Offices:
See above. Do not remove sacred threads. Do not wash the body, this is part of the funeral rite.
Autopsy/Transplants:
Post-mortems are often perceived as disrespectful and abusive and should only be performed if legally required. If necessary, then it is important to ensure that all the organs are returned to the body before the Cremation. This safeguards a peaceful existence in the afterlife. Cremations should take place as soon as possible after the death.
Sacred Books or Texts:
The Vedas Upanishads, and the Bhagavad Gita are both sacred accounts of early encounters with the gods.
A red mark placed on a woman’s forehead usually signifies that she is a Hindu.

--- SOURCE SEPARATOR ---

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/:u:/r/sites/chaplaincy-services-intranet/SitePages/Islam.aspx

Chaplaincy Services
ISLAM
Islam: Arabic word meaning “Peace”
(Muslim- meaning literally “one who submits to the will of Allah” “God”)
Islam teaches that God is the absolute owner of everything and humanity are His trustees. This world is a place of trial and testing, in preparation for death where each will be judged according to the amount of good or evil that has been performed. Each person is then rewarded or punished according to their behaviour on this earth. The framework that is used as a basis for belief for a Muslim is called the “Five Pillars of Islam”. These are obligatory acts of worship for all Muslims. They are summarized in the hadith of Gabriel. The Sunni and Shia agree on the basic details of the performance and practice of these acts, but the Shia do not refer to them by the same name. They are:
- Profession of Faith (shahada). The belief that "There is no god but God, and Muhammad is the Messenger of God" This is central to Islam.
- Prayer (salat). Muslims pray facing Mecca five times a day: at dawn, noon, mid-afternoon, sunset, and after dark.
- Alms (zakat). Giving to the poor brings blessings both to the recipient and to the giver.
- Fasting (sawm). Regular fasting for spiritual development as well as during the season of Ramadan when fasting takes place between sunrise and sunset.
- Pilgrimage (hajj). For those who are physically and financially able to make the journey to Makkah, the Hajj is a once in a lifetime duty that is the peak of their religious life.
Leadership:
The local mosque has an Imam or teacher who will preach and give guidance, especially at Friday prayers known as “Jummah”. An imam delivers a sermon (khutbah) after the prayer. Only men are expected to pray on Fridays, but women are welcome to attend.
Diet:
Lawful food is called Halal , which means that they are made, produced, manufactured, processed, and stored using machinery, equipment, and/or utensils that have been cleaned according to Islamic law (shariah). Free from any component that Muslims are prohibited from eating according to Islamic law.
Birth:
A male Muslim child is required to be circumcised as soon as possible. Often and older member of the family will whisper a verse from the Qu’ran into the baby’s ear as a first sound, and dates or honey are placed on the baby’s tongue for sweetness to be the first taste.
Death:
As with birth relatives of the dying person may whisper in their ear the articles of faith so that these are the last words he or she might hear. (“Verily we belong to Allah, and truly to Him shall we return”). They may wish to lie facing Mecca with the foot of the bed towards Mecca. The body should not be touched by non-Muslims after death. The body is covered and a light left on where the body is placed prior to burial.
Last Offices:
These should be carried out by Muslims of the same gender as the deceased.
Autopsy/Transplants:
Post-mortems are not normally allowed; the body is considered to belong to God. If a post-mortem is legally necessary, the family may be very distressed.
Funeral:
The body should be buried within 24 hours. The grave must face Mecca so must the feet of the dead person.
Sacred Book:
Qu’ran (Koran). The Divine Revelation for Almighty Allah to Mohammed the prophet.
Frequently Asked Questions
Isn’t Islam really difficult to follow?
No – it’s the basis of our life, not an extra. Once it’s in your daily routine you don’t even have to think about it. From the outside it might seem quite strict and a lot to take on, but when it’s been a part of your routine for years you don’t think of it as an extra responsibility.
How many times do you pray? What are the requirements?
Muslims are required to pray five times a day. The times at which these prayers happen is flexible, and changes depending on when the sun rises and sets. In order to pray, Muslims just need a space where they can lay out their prayer mat and carry out the rituals of prostration (kneeling face down on the floor) and prayer. Don’t worry – we won’t mind a slightly noisy environment, and we don’t need to be by ourselves, although some people may prefer to be. Some people may also prefer to take off their shoes, whereas some people think it’s fine to keep them on.
Why is there such a wide variety of ways that Islam is practised?
There is a wide variety of ways in which Islam is understood and practised. Some people are very orthodox and adhere to a strict set of rules, whereas others feel they can be more relaxed. There are also a variety of schools of thought on how the Qur’an should be interpreted. No one of these understandings is uniquely 'right' - different people simply have different views.
Our Muslim Advisors
Aicha Bahij is a lecturer in Sociology and Criminology within the Faculty of Management, Law and Social Sciences. Alongside her academic work, Aicha is an artist and researcher. Her artwork and research explore the human condition, focusing on how we express ourselves and the ways our identities, cultures, and beliefs shape both how we perceive ourselves and how others perceive us. Aicha has been serving as the Muslim Faith Advisor at the University for 8 years, where she provides support and guidance to Muslim students and staff, fostering an inclusive and understanding environment. She works to ensure that the needs of Muslims on campus are met, particularly with regard to religious practices, cultural integration and promoting wellbeing. Aicha also offers advice and support on academic and personal challenges, encouraging dialogue and awareness across the university community
Shamsuddin Ahmed is a student casework officer at the university. He has previously worked in the local authority (youth & community service, regeneration & corporate law). He has previously been an employment tribunal lay representative for nearly a decade and a Chaplain with the NHS since 2010 engaging with both patients and staffs engaging on routine and complex matters. He is a member of the West Yorkshire Police Independent Scrutiny and Advisory Panel and also has a passion in safeguarding in educational settings (primary and secondary) and is an accredited training consultant. Community empowerment and working with young people has been a prominent feature of professional life. In his spare time enjoys cycling and flying light aircraft.”

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/:u:/r/sites/chaplaincy-services-intranet/SitePages/JUDAISM.aspx

Chaplaincy Services
JUDAISM
Judaism is one of the three major monotheistic religions. (The other two are Islam and Christianity). All three faiths recognise the prophet Abraham as their ancestor, the first human to have a personal relationship with the Creator God. Known as a Patriarch.
The essential belief is that there is one Spiritual God who cannot be represented in any shape or form. The law is found within the Old Testament, the promised Messiah is still to come, and so they wait for Him.
The Sabbath is observed from Sunday on Friday until Saturday evening, and no work should be caused or carried out during this period.
Heads are usually covered, men wear a kippah, or yarmulke, a brimless cap, usually made of cloth, A woman would wear a headscarf or a wig – referred to in Yiddish as a sheitel – they signal to other people that they are married and that they comply with traditional notions that are socially acceptable.
Leadership:
In individual religious congregations or synagogues, the spiritual leader is the rabbi. Rabbis are expected to be taught in both the Talmud and the Shulkhan Arukh (Code of Jewish Law) as well as many other classical texts of Jewish scholarship. There are three different branches of Judaism: Orthodox, Conservative and Reform.
Birth:
Male babies have their bris, or circumcision on the 8 day after prayers, usually carried out by a mohel or trained doctor. This service includes special prayers and readings.
Diet:
Food must be designated as Kosher: this means that c ertain animals may not be eaten at all. Of the animals that can be eaten, the birds and mammals must be killed in accordance with Jewish law. All blood must be drained from meat and poultry or broiled out of it before it is eaten. Certain parts of permitted animals may not be eaten. Meat products and Milk or milk products must never be mixed or combined.
Death:
When a Jewish person is dying it is not permitted in Jewish law to move or touch them in any manner that may be seen to hasten death. Whilst there is no ceremony of last rites, the dying person may wish to recite the “Shema” a daily prayer for ancient Israelites found in the book of Deuteronomy. “Hear O Israel, the Lord is our God, the Lord is one. And as for you, you shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, with all your soul, and with all your strength.” According to Jewish law a dying person should never be left alone.
Burials usually take place within 24 hours of death. This is only delayed for the Sabbath. Cremation is not permitted in Jewish Law.
Autopsy/Transplants:
Post-mortem examinations are not permitted in Jewish Law except in cases of emergency or when the law demands it.
Sacred Books:
Hebrew Scriptures- The Torah (Old Testament in the Christian Bible) and the Talmud, which is a record of Rabbinic debates and the law.
Other Information:
Sabbath Observances
The Sabbath (in Hebrew, Shabbat , pronounced shah-BAHT–or in some communities, Shabbos, “SHAH-bis”) is Judaism’s most distinctive and characteristic practice. A weekly 25-hour observance, from just before sundown each Friday through to the completion of nightfall on Saturday, Shabbat is more than just a day off from working. It is a day of physical and spiritual delights that is meant to illuminate certain key concepts in the traditional Jewish perception of the world.
One constant theme in Shabbat observance across time and territory is the centrality of home life with family members and guests. Preparation for Shabbat begins as early as mid-week in some households, and its arrival is marked by the spiritual illumination of a candle-lighting ceremony. Rabbinic tradition mandates three Shabbat meals, two begun with a special kiddush (“sanctification”) recited over wine. Family meals are occasions for singing, studying, and celebrating together, as well as for consuming distinctive Shabbat foods. Nothing that requires labour, or forces another person into labour should be carried out during Shabbat.
Frequently Asked Questions
What is kosher food?
Kosher means fit or appropriate. Jewish laws state that meat and dairy should never be combined, and that only certain types of meat and fish are permissible to eat. Animals are slaughtered in a particular humane way (very similar to halal meat for Muslims). The animals that are considered non-kosher are deemed to have qualities or characteristics that do not elevate the consumer, as eating is part of a nourishment of the soul as well as the body. Eating according to a kosher regime is an opportunity to shop, cook and eat mindfully and ethically.

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/it-services-intranet/SitePages/OneDrive-storage-allocation-is-changing-to-100GB.aspx

IT Services
OneDrive storage allocation is changing to 100GB
Currently, students and staff get 5TB of storage with their OneDrive (cloud-based file storage we get as part of our University IT accounts). However, Microsoft will be making a change in September that means staff and students will each get a new allocation of 100GB of storage.
Most staff and students store data that are well within this new limit. If you are among the small number of people with data that exceed 100GB, we will contact you directly to make you aware, and to give guidance on what you need to do.
Please note that this change only affects OneDrive and does not impact on SharePoint or Teams storage.
You can see how much space you're using on your OneDrive, and identify your largest files, by following this Manage your OneDrive for work or school storage guidance from Microsoft - this page shows how you can check either via the sync client method (if you use this) or via the OneDrive website.
Note: If you are trying to view your storage usage via the OneDrive sync client method on a University device, rather than via the OneDrive website method, make sure you look at the number at the bottom left of the settings window (not the number in the centre of the window).
File management - good practice
-  Please review your OneDrive content frequently and delete any files you no longer need. This helps you to stay within your storage limit and helps with carbon footprint (even though files are stored ‘in the cloud’, this still comes with a carbon cost).
Note: When you delete files from OneDrive, they go into your OneDrive’s recycle bin for 30 days before they are deleted forever, so you have a chance to retrieve deleted files during this period if you need to.
- OneDrive should be used for your own working files only, and not business-critical files. Business critical files should be stored on SharePoint instead (in an Office 365 Group or a Microsoft team), so that other appropriate colleagues also have access when you are away or if you leave the University.
- Staff: Not sure what you should be keeping, and for how long? See the records management information on the Information Governance intranet site (an updated version of the records retention schedule is also currently in development).
