SOURCE_DOC: Chemical Storage in Laboratories Guidance - February 2024.pdf

Health and Safety
Chemical Storage in Laboratories
1. Introduction
This is a guidance document that explains how to ensure that lab users fully comply with Control of Substances Hazardous to Health (COSHH) Policy and the Dangerous Substances and Explosive Atmospheres (DSEAR) Policy, when storing reagents in laboratories.
2. Scope
This guidance applies to anyone working in any laboratory or workshop at the University where either substance hazardous to health or dangerous substances are being stored.
3. Glossary
3.1 Safety Data Sheet- All suppliers of reagents and similar products must provide a safety data sheet (SDS) with the product. This is a document that lists information relating to occupational safety and health for the use of various substances and products. SDS information may include instructions for the safe use and potential hazards associated with a particular material or product, along with spill-handling procedures. The format of all SDS should follow the requirements of GHS.
3.2 GHS- Globally Harmonized System of Classification and Labelling of Chemicals- this is an internationally agreed-upon standard managed by the United Nations that was set up to replace the assortment of hazardous material classification and labelling schemes previously used around the world.
3.3 Substance Hazardous to Health- Defined in the COSHH Regulations. The term refers to any substances that are hazardous to human health. COSHH regulation 4 gives a more detailed definition.
3.4 Dangerous Substance- Defined in the DSEAR Regulations. The term refers to any vapour, gas, or dust that if mixed with air might form an explosive atmosphere. DSEAR Regulation 2 gives a more detailed definition.
4. Responsibilities
All individuals working in laboratories are expected to follow this guidance.
5. General Principles for Storing Chemicals in Labs
General
5.1 All substances that are used in laboratories and workshops must be stored in a safe manner.
5.2 The SDS should always be consulted if further information is required on storage and chemical incompatibilities of a particular chemical substance.
5.3 There should not be an excessive about of reagents stored in labs. There should be no more than 10 litres flammable liquids stored on benches.
5.3 There should be no more than 50 litres of flammable liquid stored in any one lab. Any excess should be stored in the designated solvent store.
5.4 Breakable containers, particularly those containing liquids, should be stored below shoulder height. To reduce the risk of spillages, escape of fumes etc. it important that all containers are sealed correctly, this may be by means of ensuring a screw top lid is tight. (N.B. if the chemical gives off a gas ensure a ‘breather cap’ is fitted to prevent an explosion).
5.5 Bottles containing hazardous substances must never be carried by the top. Always carry Winchester bottles (2.5 litres) in carriers or baskets that offer support to the base of the bottle.
5.6 Any hazardous substances that are no longer used must be disposed of appropriately.
Labelling of Checmical Containers
5.7 New reagents should, were possible, be kept, in their original containers. The original labelling must be preserved on those containers.
5.8 All hazardous substances should have a label on them identifying their hazard category (e.g. flammable, corrosive, oxidising, toxic etc.). The label should be compatible with the GHS for labelling.
5.9 When reagents are transferred to new containers or if new solutions or mixtures are created, they must be clearly labelled with their contents, the date when the mixture/solution was created, the name of the person who created and if possible, a date when the contents will be discarded.
Segregation
5.10 It may be necessary to segregate incompatible substances to prevent dangerous interactions.
5.11 The Appendix has a summary of hazardous substances that should be segregated is listed below to assist storage.
5.12 Issues worthy of special note as needing to be avoided include:
5.12.1. Flammable solvents must never be stored with strong oxidising agents (e.g. sodium hypochlorite – bleach, iodine and other halogens, hydrogen peroxide, nitric acid, potassium permanganate etc.) reducing agents (e.g. Sodium borohydride, lithium aluminium hydride etc.) or concentrated acids (e.g.
concentrated sulphuric or hydrochloric acids).
5.12.2. Concentrated acids and alkalis must be stored separately; any accidental mixing of concentrated materials may generate large quantities of heat and fumes.
Designated Storage
5.13 A number of different types of specialised cabinets are available for the specific use of chemical storage, these include:
5.13.1. Acid Cabinets – These are made of acid resistant materials and should contain a tray, to contain any leakage or spillage.
5.13.2. Flammable Solvent Cabinets – These are made of fire-resistant material with a minimum fire resistance of 30 minutes as required by BS EN 14470-1:2023.
5.13.3. Ventilated Cabinets – These are cabinets fitted with an extraction system; The fumes are extracted by forced ventilation. This type of cabinet should be used to store materials such as mercaptans and amines which give off hazardous vapours and in particularly those give off strong smelling vapours . In the absence of ventilated cabinets, noxious hazardous substances can be stored in sealed secondary containers, when stored in this manner the containers should only be opened in a fume cupboard.
5.14 Use of Fume Cupboards – Fume cupboards are not designed or intended for the storage of hazardous substances. The working surfaces of fume cupboards should therefore be kept clear of materials and containers when these are not needed for the ongoing work activities. In addition, the excess storage of hazardous substances in fume cupboards disrupts the airflow resulting in a lower level of protection to users.
Storage of specific hazardous substances
5.15 Flammable Solvents - e.g. alcohols, toluene, hexane. The vapours given off by flammable liquid/solvents are often the main source of danger. This vapour is very susceptible to ignition by naked flames, sparks from electrical switches, motors etc. or from sparks produced electrostatically by friction. Precautions must therefore be taken to prevent contact between any of these and the concentrated vapours of flammable liquids.
5.15.1. Flammable solvents must be stored in specialised lockable metal flammable cabinets; these must be clearly labelled and positioned away from doors or other means of escape from the laboratory. It should be noted that no more than 50 litres of a flammable liquids may be kept in any one laboratory room to reduce the risk of a serious laboratory fire, the flammable .
5.15.2. Containers of flammable solvents used during teaching/research (i.e. those kept on the bench) should not exceed 500 ml (this volume can be easily contained should an accident/fire occur) and the solvent must be kept in a suitable closed vessel. An exception to this is where the experiment being undertaken has a direct feed from a Winchester.
5.15.3. Flammable solvents must not be stored in fume cupboards or vented cabinets, unless there is a heat activated damper on the extraction ductwork. This will prevent spread the fire to other parts of the building via the ventilation ducting. The extraction fan will exacerbate the spread of fire if there is no damper.
5.16 Chlorinated solvents (e.g. chloroform, dichloromethane (DCM)
trichloroethylene) are best stored in ventilated cabinets separately from flammable (non-chlorinated) solvents because if they get involved in a fire, they will give off toxic fumes and gases. They should not be stored with alkali metals such as lithium, potassium, or sodium, since any mixing can cause an explosion. They can be stored in metal containers if ventilated storage is not available.
5.17 Strong Acids and Strong Alkalis
Strong Acids and strong alkalis will react strongly with each other and must be stored separately; any accidental mixing of may generate large quantities of heat and fumes. They can be stored in a vented or metal cabinet so long as they are in a containment tray to prevent any spillages. However consideration must be given to the effects of corrosive fumes on any metal in the fittings and construction of the container.
The use of ventilated cabinets is recommended where possible, allowing the removal of fumes at source. All containers/bottle tops must be suitably sealed to avoid unnecessary leakage of fumes.
5.18 Oxidisers (e.g. peroxides, perchlorates, and nitrates)
Oxidising substances should be stored in metal cabinets and away from any organic matter such as wood and paper (N.B. oxidising agents should never be stored in a wooden cabinet!). Oxidising agents must never be stored with flammable solvents or reducing agents since fires and explosions can result after any spillage, even without a naked flame or heat.
Perchloric acid is an extremely strong oxidising agent particularly in concentrated form; this acid can react explosively with organic materials. It should ideally be stored separately on a metal tray of sand within a cabinet, away from organic materials or dehydrating agents such as sulphuric acid.
You should always check with the SDS if in doubt.
6. Monitoring and review
The application of this guidance will be checked during the usual audit procedure.
The findings of these audits will be fed back to the relevant H&S Panels.

SOURCE_DOC: Corridors and Escape Routes.pdf

Health and Safety
Corridors and Escape Routes
Introduction
In order to safeguard corridors and exit routes that are used as part of building evacuation procedures it is essential that they remain free from combustible materials that could hinder evacuation procedures.
The Regulatory Reform (Fire Safety) Order 2005, (referred to as ‘the Fire Safety Order’), Building Regulations and associated Approved Documents require escape routes and corridors be kept free from obstructions and that they are able to be used safely at all material times. To achieve this it is essential we have certain rules and guidance to ensure the University meets its statutory obligations.
In addition to fire safety legislation the fact that we must maintain safe corridors and exit routes is also recognised within legislation and guidance documents. A fire in a corridor or staircase could have devastating consequences with people becoming trapped within their offices, work or study areas.
Scope
This code shall apply to all University buildings, owned, leased or managed.
Furniture
Furniture must not be placed in corridors; these are escape routes and must be kept sterile.
Where areas within corridors designated as quiet study/social spaces it must be constructed of a low-risk material e.g. metal or solid wood. Plastic seating should be avoided. Where soft furnishings are used, they must conform to the Furniture and Furnishings (Fire) (Safety) Regulations 1988 (as amended 1989, 1993 and 2010).
Unwanted furniture must not be discarded into corridors, staircases or lift lobbies, not even temporarily whilst awaiting collection for disposal. The Property Services ancillary services should be contacted for disposal.
Pigeonholes and Lockers
It has been common practice in the past to have wooden pigeon holes for student work submissions, correspondence and staff mail etc. this practice is not encouraged but where appropriate can be continued, however only metal units should be used, it is also a requirement that the top of the unit/pigeon hole has a sloping top so as to prevent it from being used for storage.
It is also noted that lockers have been placed within a number of corridors, particularly outside laboratories, numbers of these need to be restricted and their positioning must be appropriate. They must also be constructed of metal with a sloping top.
The contents of lockers must be restricted, and should be limited to outdoor clothing, books, papers etc. and personal effects e.g. handbags. No flammable substances should be stored within, not even on a temporary basis, prohibited substances for storage in lockers in corridors include; Chemicals, Samples, Biological Agents or other such materials, compressed gas high risk liquids and gasses, flammable or highly flammable materials. All lockers must be locked when in use.
The Head of HSW must be consulted prior to the placing of both lockers and pigeon holes. Locations must be appropriate, by this they should only be placed where there is adjacent detection, they must not be placed within lift lobbies or staircases (not even temporarily). They should not be placed in areas where there is only one means of escape e.g. a ‘dead end’ corridor serving two or three offices.
It must be further noted that lockers within corridors are for the temporary storage of student personal belonging, these are not to be used as a personal long term storage facility. Any locker found locked at the end of the academic day may have the lock removed.
Coat Hooks
Coat hooks must not be placed in any corridor used on a means of escape.
Bookcases and Filing Cabinets
Bookcases and Filing Cabinets must not be stored or located within any escape route or protected corridor. Corridors are circulation areas and not office extensions.
Display Materials and Decorations
Displays are usually positioned within entrance lobbies and atria etc. they usually incorporate paper, plastics and cardboard, all of which will assist in developing a rapid fire. To reduce risks the following must be considered:
- Minimise the display size and try to position them so they will not obstruct exits,
- Treat display material with a flame retardant spray where appropriate,
- Utilise display cabinets if possible;
- Keep displays away from the vicinity of any heat source including light fittings and heaters/radiators,
- Ensure there are no ignition sources in the area,
Permanent and semi-permanent displays including the displaying of artwork associated with student assignments, exhibitions etc. require separate consideration, and for these instances the Head of HSW should be consulted.
Notice Boards
Notice boards within rooms may be up to 3 metres in length, if more than one is required within the same location there should be a 1 metre gap separating them.
In corridors and escape routes however they should conform to class 0 (zero); this means that the notice board must be glazed or polycarbonate used, the frame must be aluminium or hardwood and the door/cover must be lockable. The core of the notice board must also meet class 0 standards.
Temporary Signs and Notices
Temporary signs used internally within buildings offering directions to rooms or conferences etc. must not be placed on walls, pillars, windows or doors. Flame retardant polycarbonate display holders have been installed within lift lobbies for this purpose. Where there are shortfalls identified a request should be made to Property Services for additional holders.
Event organisers must ensure they include building floor plans etc. with any joining instructions to alleviate the necessity of plastering walls and doors with directional signage.
Any temporary notice found on a fire door, within a protected route or an escape route will be removed.
10 Corridor Seating Areas
In some buildings the corridor is of such a width that some have been utilised as a seating/rest area or Student write up area. As these areas still form part of the escape route a number of precautions must be adopted:
- The amount of furniture must be restricted, guidance should be sought from the Head of HSW, paragraph 3 relating to Furniture must be observed.
- Seating areas must not be positioned in corridors where escape is only possible in one direction; this would have the effect of blocking off the corridor.
- Inner-inner rooms must not be created (i.e. to get to an office you must not have to pass through two rooms to get to it),
- Corridors and access lobbies must not become ‘rooms’.
Electrical Equipment
Electrical equipment including photocopiers, printers must not be installed on escape routes, in protected corridors, lift lobbies or staircases.
Vending machines must not be installed on any escape route, lift lobby or within staircase enclosures.
Unwanted electrical equipment must not be discarded into corridors, staircases or lift lobbies, not even temporarily whilst awaiting collection for disposal. The Property Services ancillary services should be contacted for disposal, equipment must remain in offices etc. until collected.
Fire Doors
Fire doors form part of the building’s passive fire protection, they are not notice boards. Any posters, directional signage, or notices etc. that are displayed on a fire door will be removed and disposed of.
Whilst care is taken to identify building compartment lines it may not always be practicable to increase these, as a compromise and to mitigate the migration of by-products of combustion e.g. smoke and flames, where doors open onto the escape route they should be fire rated to a minimum standard of FD30s.
Internal office doors that do not open onto an escape route need not be fire rated unless they form part of a compartment line, plant room, kitchenette, server room or other room of high risk.

SOURCE_DOC: Fire Evacuation Procedures v1.2.pdf

Health and Safety
Fire Evacuation Procedures and Assembly Points
(Version 1.2)
October 2009 v 1.2
Introduction
The purpose of this document is to introduce the evacuation strategy which is adopted throughout all University academic and student residence buildings and give guidance in certain areas to facilitate a smooth evacuation process. This document aims to remove any confusion on the hearing of fire alarm systems.
The enclosed plans will assist in the identification of an appropriate assembly point with varying locations for different University buildings. In addition, routes are indicated dependent upon which exit from a building is used. All personnel should familiarise themselves with the most appropriate assembly point for the building in which they normally work, and where applicable any additional assembly points for their building should the normal exit route be obstructed.
Any queries should be directed to the University Fire Safety Officer who is contactable on extension 5764.
University of Bradford Fire Safety Policy Statement
The University of Bradford recognises that fire can have a devastating effect on life as well as its business and activities and is totally committed to providing a safe environment for study and work. The University of Bradford will ensure so far as is reasonably practicable that all staff, students, contractors and visitors are protected from the risks and effects of fire, and will take such general fire precautions as may reasonably be required to ensure their safety.
Emergencies require people to react in certain ways to ensure their safety; this includes safe evacuation procedures from buildings. In order to give people the knowledge they need to undertake this, fire drills are conducted at least once every academic year. The fire drills will also help to highlight any deficiencies or defects in our procedures.
It is the responsibility of each individual, whether staff, student, visitor or contractor to cooperate with the emergency procedures implemented by the University. This document forms part of the schedule of the Fire Safety Policy.
Within this Evacuation document, there are a number of foreseeable scenarios along with an explanation of the action to be followed in each case, please note set procedures for predictable situations cannot be laid down, as there are too many variables of a given situation, which may dictate how the incident and evacuation are best handled. As a general principle one of progressive horizontal evacuation should be used, i.e. putting as much distance between yourself and the fire whilst remaining on the same floor level, this is particularly the case for persons with a disability.
Staff are once again reminded that under health and safety legislation we are all responsible for our own safety and that of others who may be affected by our actions or in-actions. At no time should people act in a reckless or negligent manner that could lead to a fire situation or obstruction of fire exits or exit routes, nor should things provided in the interests of safety be tampered with or misused.
Action to be taken if you discover a fire
- Raise the alarm – this can be done by shouting and or operating one of the red break glass call points, these are generally located on exit routes or the way out of a room next to the door.
- If it is safe to do so close any open windows and doors
- If it is safe to do so and you have been trained in the use of fire extinguishers, then tackle the fire by using an appropriate extinguisher provided, keep your back to the exit route and if the fire extinguisher is not making any difference and the fire continues to increase in size leave the room immediately.
- Leave by the nearest available exit, this may not be the point at which you entered the building.
- Do not Stop to collect personal belongings,
Action to take upon hearing the fire alarm or on receipt of a verbal instruction to evacuate the building
- Leave the premises by the nearest available exit, this is not necessarily the way you entered the building.
- Once outside the building proceed to the nearest assembly point.
- Move away from the building, (in a fire situation glass shatters and concrete explodes).
- Do not re-enter the building until given the all clear by security personnel.
Please note; failing to proceed to an assembly point can be both dangerous to the individual and obstructive to the Emergency Services.
Additionally, it is the responsibility of each individual to follow the evacuation protocol promptly upon hearing the fire alarm, in the event of a bomb threat or similar type of emergency it may be necessary to initiate this procedure verbally.
Disabled persons
Persons with a disability should proceed to a refuge at the furthest point within a building from the fire. The majority of refuges are located in stairwells; their location is indicated by appropriate signage.
As an example of the procedure in the event of an alarm activation, if we look at the facilities and lay out of the Richmond Building, if the alarm activation was on the Richmond wing, evacuees should, providing it is safe, proceed to the central lift lobby initially, then to the end of the Carlton wing. In this location, there is a refuge with voice communication facilities, if it was not safe to proceed to the Central lift lobby or the Carlton wing because of the location of the fire, then the lift lobby at the end of the Richmond wing could be used.
The communication facilities within the refuge can be used to contact Security, who will advise the person to either: a) Remain at the refuge until the alarm has been investigated, b) Commence descent if able, c) Arrange for assistance to be sent,
A new system for voice communications is being installed in other buildings, this system will allow Emergency Wardens to communicate with both security and directly to the person waiting at the refuge.
Personal Emergency Evacuation Plans (PEEP’s)
PEEP’s are personalised evacuation plans for people with a disability whether permanent or temporary who would need assistance to evacuate a building in the event of an emergency, particularly where this is mobility related, as lifts should not be generally used in emergencies. Any person who does not have one of these but feels they would need assistance to evacuate should contact the University Fire Safety Officer, who can be reached by email - S.W.Wiggins@Bradford.ac.uk ).
Phased Evacuation
The Richmond Building is currently the only building within the University portfolio that has phased evacuation; this is a process of evacuating only certain parts of the building to limit the disruption. The way in which this currently works is that the floor where an alarm has been received would evacuate along with the floor directly above, e.g. fire alarm activated on E floor Carlton, alarm sounds on E and F floors of Carlton only. Persons in these areas should then evacuate the building by the nearest available exit.
Individuals with responsibilities
Academic Staff – Staff who are teaching at the time of an incident should try to ensure that their teaching area is evacuated by all students. They should make everyone under their guidance aware that the fire alarm has sounded and of the need to evacuate the building immediately. In the event of their being someone within their charge who has a disability, they should assist the student in locating their safe refuge point. Academic staff may choose to leave the building or stay with the student until the alarm has been investigated and the all clear given or until assistance arrives. All disabled persons should have been given a Personal Emergency Evacuation Plan (PEEP) and should know what action to take in the event of an emergency.
Students – Students are responsible for their own safety and will be expected to follow any instruction or guidance given during any emergency.
Technical/Support staff – Support staff should follow the guidance contained within this document, however it is expected that they would endeavour to ensure any person present within their work area who is unfamiliar with the procedure or suffering from a disability, is given guidance and assistance on the actions to take during the emergency incident where appropriate.
Emergency Wardens – These are University staff who have volunteered to provide assistance and support during an emergency. They will guide evacuees to exit routes and conduct an internal sweep of the area where they normally work to ensure occupants have evacuated. Outside the building wardens will guide evacuees to assembly points and away from any potential danger.
Emergency wardens will liaise with Security and the Emergency Services, and assist in the evacuation of persons within emergency refuges should it be necessary.
All personnel are respectfully reminded that wardens are volunteers and play an important role within the evacuation procedure, they should not be give any verbal abuse for the task they have been asked to undertake, persons who are either abusive or fail to evacuate the building will be dealt with severely through the appropriate channels.
Security Personnel – Security personnel will be investigating the alarm activation and should not be relied upon to assist in evacuation, they will also give the instruction that it is safe to re-enter a building following an emergency.

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/health-safety-and-wellbeing-intranet/documents
SOURCE_DOC: Pandemic Policy Appendix A Infectious Diseases.pdf

Health and Safety
INFECTIOUS AND COMMUNICABLE DISEASES
1. Introduction
Please Note, This document has been produced to supplement the Pandemic Incident Policy.
Whilst major outbreaks of serious diseases and infections are rare, the University must consider known disease risks and have contingencies and plans in place to mitigate the associated risks that are likely to cause disruption to the daily operations of the University; the guidance and procedures must also seek to support those who may be affected, reduce the potential for transmission and protect staff, students, and visitors. In addition, the procedural document outlines the University’s role with the UK Health Security Agency (UKHSA) in assisting them in the discharge of their duties related to managing communicable disease and potential outbreaks as well as ensuring the University meets its duty of care.
There are several ways that the University may be alerted to a student or member of staff suffering from a communicable disease; one of the primary considerations is to find out what type of disease the individual may be suffering with, in addition it would be prudent to be able to identify (in the case of a student) where they are living as this may have a knock-on effect with other residents with whom they may be sharing accommodation with.
2. Definitions
- Communicable Disease - Any disease that is transmissible by infection or contagion directly or through the agency of a vector;
- Notifiable Disease – A disease with significant public health implications, typically a highly infectious disease, for which the diagnosing clinician has a statutory responsibility to notify the correct body or person e.g. Public Health;
- Public Health – Public health departments have a key role in the management of infectious and communicable diseases, ensuring that there are adequate disease prevention and surveillance programmes, and in the prevention of secondary spread through contact tracing;
- Possible case – Case not requiring public health action; the individual has provided a sample for testing and the clinician is awaiting test results;
- Probable case - Clinical diagnosis of a communicable or infectious disease where the clinician considers that an infection is the most likely diagnosis;
- Confirmed case - Case requiring public health action;
- Outbreak - An outbreak or incident may be defined as an event in which two or more people experiencing a similar illness are linked in time or place.
3. Responsibilities
Any member of the University community including staff, student, contractor, or visitor who believes they may have come into contact with a person or persons that have a communicable or infectious disease must seek to confirm a diagnosis of their suspected condition.
Likewise, any member of the University (staff, student, contractor, or visitor) who knows that they have a communicable disease has an obligation to minimise physical contact with other members of the University community.
Any member of the University community including staff, student, contractor or visitor who have been diagnosed with (or suspects a diagnosis of) a communicable disease that could be spread through routine contact must observe any medical advice that may have been issued on appropriate isolation until they no longer have the communicable disease or the communicable disease is no longer transmissible through ‘routine’ contact or as determined by a medical professional.
Prior to returning to studies/work, it would be normal practice for the University member to provide a return to work note in line with Sickness Reporting Policy or Extenuating Circumstances Policy.
Depending upon the circumstances, the response to an alert of an infectious or communicable disease must be measured and appropriate, the seriousness of any report will help to determine the numbers of people that may need to be involved. The Head of Health, Safety and Security or nominated deputy will be integral in managing any outbreak; other key stake holders, dependent upon the infected person, will include the Director of People and Campus Services or the Director of Student and Academic Services, or their nominated deputies.
3.1 Key Stake holders
- Director of People and Campus Services; The Director of People and Campus Services must be informed of all reports of communicable and infectious disease reports, this will ensure a measured response can be taken with any staff alerting requirements;
- Academic Registrar and Director of Student and Academic Services (SAS); In a similar vein as that of the Director of People and Campus Services, the same process would be followed for alerting and responding to issues relating to a student outbreak;
- Deans and Directors; Must ensure that staff and students within their respective areas are alerted to any outbreak and provide them with information as may be required, consideration will need to be given to other staff and students studying or teaching on the same course;
- Chief Executive Officer University Bradford Union; Will be responsible for disseminating information within the student’s union. Alerting within the student community must include any social contacts including bars, sports teams etc.
- Head of Health, Safety and Security (HSS); The Head of HSS will act in the capacity of coordinator, ensuring appropriate persons are alerted accordingly, also consult with external bodies such as Public Health etc.
- Head of Counselling; Will need to make staff available to discuss any concerns or anxiety amongst the student or staff community;
- Associate Director of PR and Communications; Collaborating with the Director of People and Campus Services and or Director of Student and Academic Services issues communications as may be required ensuring accurate information is disseminated appropriately and timely;
4. Infection Prevention and Control
The aim of prevention and control of infections should aim to interrupt the chain of transmission, to aid in this several safeguards can be implemented and conducted as a collective, these include institutional measure and individual responsibilities.
Those who become ill whilst at University with a suspected case of an infectious disease should exclude themselves immediately. (Please see Table below).
- Infection: Chickenpox
  - Exclusion Period: At least 5 days from onset of rash and until all blisters have crusted over
- Infection: Coronavirus
  - Exclusion Period: Until recovered or when no longer have a high temperature
- Infection: Diarrhoea and vomiting
  - Exclusion Period: Staff and students can return 48 hours after diarrhoea and vomiting have stopped
- Infection: Diptheria*
  - Exclusion Period: Exclusion is essential, consult with your UKHSA HPT
  - Additional: Until recovered
- Infection: Hepatitis A
  - Exclusion Period: Exclude until 7 days after onset of jaundice (or 7 days after symptom onset if no jaundice)
- Infection: Impetigo
  - Exclusion Period: Until lesions are crusted or healed, or 48 hours after starting antibiotic treatment
- Infection: Measles
  - Exclusion Period: 4 days from onset of rash and well enough
- Infection: Meningococcal meningitis*, septicaemia*
  - Exclusion Period: Until recovered
- Infection: Meningitis* due to other bacteria
  - Exclusion Period: Until recovered
- Infection: Meningitis (viral)
  - Exclusion Period: No need to isolate
- Infection: Mumps*
  - Exclusion Period: 5 days after onset of swelling
- Infection: Rubella* (German measles)
  - Exclusion Period: 5 days from onset of rash
- Infection: Scabies
  - Exclusion Period: Can return after first treatment
- Infection: Scarlet fever*
  - Exclusion Period: Exclude until 24 hours after starting antibiotic treatment
- Infection: Tuberculosis* (TB)
  - Exclusion Period: Until at least 2 weeks after the start of effective antibiotic treatment (if pulmonary TB)
  - Note: Exclusion not required for non-pulmonary or latent TB infection. Always consult your local HPT before disseminating information.
- Infection: Whooping cough*
  - Exclusion Period: 2 days from starting antibiotic treatment, or 21 days from onset of symptoms if no antibiotics

 * Denotes the infection is notifiable to the UKHSA health protection team
Prompt exclusion of staff and or students who are unwell with an infectious disease is essential to prevent the spread within the University community. Should this be necessary and the individual is unable to drive or get themselves home independently the Occupational Health rooms within Richmond should be used for the purpose of isolation (E Floor Link room E86) until arrangements can be made for individuals to be transferred.
4.1 Hand Hygiene
Hand washing is one of the most important ways of controlling the spread of infections, especially those that cause diarrhoea and/or vomiting and respiratory infections.
All staff, students and visitors should wash their hands after using the toilet, before eating or handling food, remember toilet paper is porous!
In addition to the hand washing facilities widely available across campus the University has also installed hand sanitising units, it should be noted however that alcohol hand sanitiser is not effective against norovirus where thorough handwashing with soap and water is more effective.
4.2 Cleaning
The University employs many dedicated and trained cleaning staff. Good standards of general housekeeping are maintained across the estate with appropriate colour coding of cleaning equipment for the different variety of tasks undertaken to prevent cross contamination.
Keeping the workplace clean reduces the risk of the spread of infection and can play a part in reducing sickness amongst the University community, especially those surfaces that are frequently touched.
4.3 Sanitary Facilities
Within all toilets on campus hand wash basins with warm running water along with a mild liquid soap are readily available along with hand driers.
Toilet paper is also available in each cubicle, and within female toilets provisions have been made for the safe disposal of sanitary products. The University also has several accessible toilets across campus.
All areas are cleaned regularly throughout the day.
4.4 Ventilation
Ventilation is the process of introducing fresh air into indoor spaces while removing stale air. Letting fresh air into indoor spaces can help remove air that contains virus particles and prevent the spread of respiratory infections.
In addition to opening windows mechanical ventilation has been installed in many areas across campus, these units are maintained by the University mechanical team with filter changes undertaken at intervals to help capture contaminants within the air.
Fresh air within occupied spaces can help to reduce concentrations of respiratory particles thus lowering the risk of transmission to others.
The risk of catching or passing on respiratory infections can be higher in certain places, and is increased when someone with an infection breathes, speaks, coughs or sneezes. These actions release tiny particles which can contain the virus.
5. Reducing the Spread of Respiratory Diseases
5.1 Respiratory Cough Hygiene
Coughs and sneezes spread diseases. Covering the nose and mouth during sneezing and coughing can reduce the spread of infections. More specifically:
- Cover nose and mouth with a tissue when coughing and sneezing, and dispose of used tissue in non-healthcare risk waste bin and perform hand hygiene;
- Cough or sneeze into the inner elbow (upper sleeve) if no tissues are available, rather than into the hand;
- Keep contaminated hands away from the mucous membranes of the eyes and nose;
- Carry out hand hygiene after contact with respiratory secretions and contaminated objects and materials.
6. Know the Symptoms
Respiratory infections can spread quickly between people as we often experience with seasonal flu and more recently the COVID-19 pandemic. It is therefore important that staff, students, and managers are aware of the symptoms so that action can be taken to reduce the risk of spreading infections to others.
The symptoms of COVID-19 and other respiratory infections are similar, and so it may not be possible to differentiate between COVID-19, flu or another infection based on symptoms alone.
Typical symptoms of COVID-19, flu and other common respiratory infections include:
- Continuous cough;
- High temperature, fever, or chills;
- Loss of, or change in, your normal sense of taste or smell;
- Shortness of breath;
- Unexplained tiredness, or lack of energy;
- Muscle aches or pains that are not due to exercise;
- Not wanting to eat or not feeling hungry;
- Headache that is unusual or longer lasting than usual;
- Sore throat, stuffy or runny nose;
- Diarrhoea, feeling sick or being sick.
Some people may continue to cough or feel tired after other symptoms have improved, but this does not mean they remain infectious.
7. Actions to Take
If a member of staff or a student is unwell with symptoms of a respiratory infection the following guidance should be considered:
Try to stay at home and avoid contact with other people.
If people have symptoms of a respiratory infection, such as COVID-19, and have a high temperature or do not feel well enough to go to work or carry out normal activities, they should try to stay at home and avoid contact with others until they no longer have a high temperature (if they had one) or until they no longer feel unwell.
It is particularly important to avoid close contact with anyone who they know is at higher risk of becoming seriously unwell if they are infected with COVID-19 or other respiratory infections, especially those whose immune system means that they are at higher risk of serious illness, despite vaccination.
Line managers should, following discussions with the individuals, consider whether they are well enough to be able to work from home temporarily.
Covering the mouth and nose when people cough or sneeze; wash hands frequently with soap and water for 20 seconds or use hand sanitiser after coughing and sneezing.
Within the workplace regularly cleaning of frequently touched surfaces, such as door handles and handrails, door push plates etc. should be promoted.
If individuals think they may have COVID-19 they may choose to purchase a test kit, in the event of the result being positive for COVID-19, try to stay at home and avoid contact with other people for 5 days after the day you took your test. After this time, most people will no longer be infectious.
Staff who are able and well enough to work from home should be encouraged to do so, once the high temperature has subsided, they can return to work if well enough.
8. Vaccinations
Vaccines are a very effective way at preventing serious illness, particularly COVID-19 and flu, and whilst the University recognises individuals’ rights to decide whether they wish to avail themselves of the various vaccines available e.g. flu, measles, mumps, rubella, COVID-19 etc. they are to be encouraged and the University will support staff to get their vaccine when offered, this includes during normal working hours.
9. Safe Management of Blood and Body Fluids
Any spillages of blood, faeces, saliva, vomit, nasal and eye discharges should be cleaned immediately and appropriate Personal Protective Equipment (PPE) should be worn e.g. gloves, apron, splash goggles (if appropriate).
Disposable paper towels or cloths should be used to clean up blood and body fluid spills. The University has also provided spillage kit, these are available from the cleaning team and security and can be used for bodily fluids such as blood, vomit, and urine.

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/health-safety-and-wellbeing-intranet/documents
SOURCE_DOC: Portable Electrical Equipment.pdf

Health and Safety
1. Introduction
In order to safeguard corridors and exit routes that are used as part of building evacuation procedures it is essential that they remain free from combustible materials that could hinder evacuation procedures.
The Regulatory Reform (Fire Safety) Order 2005, (referred to as ‘the Fire Safety Order’), Building Regulations and associated Approved Documents require escape routes and corridors be kept free from obstructions and that they are able to be used safely at all material times. To achieve this it is essential we have certain rules and guidance to ensure the University meets its statutory obligations.
In addition to fire safety legislation the fact that we must maintain safe corridors and exit routes is also recognised within legislation and guidance documents. A fire in a corridor or staircase could have devastating consequences with people becoming trapped within their offices, work or study areas.
2. Scope
This code shall apply to all University buildings, owned, leased or managed.
3. Purpose
This code of practice is to clarify and issue guidance on the safe use of domestic type electrical equipment.
To enhance the existing Energy Saving guidance issued by Property Services and to support the Electrical and Fire Safety Policies, the indiscriminate use of domestic types of electrical equipment including portable fan heaters is not permitted. These are dangerous when no control over their positioning or use is applied.
The paragraphs below contain the guidance approved and issued by the Health, Safety & Wellbeing Committee.
4. Portable Space Heaters
Statistically this type of equipment is amongst the biggest single cause of fires nationally with over 1,700 fires being attributable to portable space heaters and over 5000 for other electrical appliances.
Where people feel that the fixed heating within their offices is insufficient, they should make a report to Property Services who will endeavour to investigate the matter within 5 working days.
If Property Services are unable to attend to the problem within this timescale or the problem has arisen due to a failure in the heating system that cannot be easily and quickly resolved, then a number of Portable Heaters may be made available by them for short-term loan. However, it must be noted that numbers may have to be restricted to ensure the electrical infrastructure can cope with the increased loading.
If the problem is one that cannot be resolved without major works being undertaken, and urgent need for heat within offices, classrooms is required then in order to comply with health and safety guidelines on minimum temperatures within workspaces oil-filled radiator type heaters may be used.
Oil filled radiators should be used in accordance with the manufacturer’s instructions/recommendations and need to be sited against a wall to prevent any obstructions in circulation spaces and to remove the risk of any trip hazard which may be caused by trailing leads.
Oil filled radiators must not be covered with items such as clothing, towels, etc. Anyone found to be contravening this instruction faces having his or her heater removed. Heaters must also fall within the Schools’ or Directorates Portable Appliance Testing (PAT) regime.
To ensure all users of the space benefit from the additional heat portable heaters should not be placed under desks.
At the end of the working day and in the event of a fire alarm activation, all heaters ‘must be switched off’ at the mains. This will help to reduce the fire risk and will contribute to the reduction of the carbon footprint and energy conservation at the University.
Oil filled radiators can if deemed necessary be purchased centrally through Property Services by contacting their help desk.
In order to comply with current University policy on electrical appliances personnel are respectfully reminded that no personal electrical appliances should be brought into the place of work.
5. Toasters
It has been noted during audits of various University buildings that there is an exceptional amount of domestic type electrical equipment in use throughout the University. There are currently University policies in place that restrict this but which would appear to be ignored; we need to ensure as an organisation the message is communicated clearly to all personnel.
Toasters are the cause of at least one evacuation per month, on the surface this does not sound much, but we need to look at other factors;
- Each time we have an alarm activation we must evacuate. An evacuation causes disruption to students and their lectures, experiments etc. In addition with too many false alarms it breeds complacency, the onetime people need to evacuate urgently will be the one time people ignore it.
- Not only is the alarm an unwanted and frustrating disruption but there is also an associated cost, no estimate has been made of this but if we were to work on the basis of one false alarm per month the potential cost could be astronomical due to several factors; Staff downtime, Demonstrators down time, Cost of abandoned experiments, Potential for bio-hazards because of experiments, Disruption to planned assessments, Disruption to exams – with associated consequences, Loss of resources, Staff investigating the cause of an alarm,
If we multiply person time by several hundred to account for occupancy evacuation, and take 10 minutes of everybody’s time in monetary terms from the Vice Chancellor down, the cost of a false alarm is into several thousands of pounds.
A decision has therefore been taken to restrict the use of toasters to designated areas only; these will be approved kitchens only; small tea making areas within offices do not qualify.
The room must be checked to ensure it is suitable from a fire safety perspective and to ensure the appropriate levels of detection are installed, Property Services electrical team can advise on the levels of detection, alternatively the Head of HSW can be contacted. Kitchenettes must have a closable door fitted with an automatic closer, if there are any other openings e.g. shutters, these must also be closable and not of the open grill type, this will then prevent any smoke from burnt toast entering the circulation areas unnecessarily and generating unwanted fire alarm signals.
When toasters are being used those using them must never leave them unattended, crumb trays must be emptied at least weekly.
6. Kettles and Coffee Machines
Other equipment that appears prolific is that of kettles etc. a large number of offices have more than one. Kettles and such equipment must be restricted to one and should only be located within designated areas such as kitchenettes. Where no such facilities exist then kettles within an office must be positioned such that is will not affect the fire detection equipment, in addition there must be no more than one.
7. Microwaves
Microwaves are restricted items and must be in kitchenettes only, microwaves have been the cause of a number of fires at the University. When staff are using microwaves they must never be left unattended.

SOURCE_DOC: Applying for a visit visa to study guidance.pdf

International Student Support and UKVI Compliance
A GUIDE TO MAKING A VISIT VISA APPLICATION FOR STUDY
Visa Nationals – MUST APPLY FOR PRIOR ENTRY CLEARANCE
You will need to apply for Entry Clearance as a visitor if you are a Visa National. Nationals of certain countries are classed as Visa Nationals; this means you must apply for a visa before you enter the UK. If you arrive in the UK without a visa, you will not be allowed to enter, and you will be expected to return home.
There is a list of countries whose citizens are Visa Nationals on the UK Visas & Immigration website: https://www.gov.uk/guidance/immigration-rules/immigration-rules-appendix-v-visitor 
Non-visa Nationals – DO NOT HAVE TO APPLY FOR PRIOR ENTRY CLEARANCE (IF COMING FOR UP TO 6 MONTHS, BUT IT IS RECOMMENDED THAT THEY DO)
You are a non-visa national if you are: a national or citizen of any country that is NOT listed on the above link (including EU, EAA and Swiss Nationals); or: a British overseas territories citizen; a British overseas citizen; a British national (overseas); a British protected person; or a British subject.
Being a Non-Visa National means that you are not required to have prior entry clearance if you are coming to the UK for up to 6 months.
Non-visa nationals can make an Entry Clearance application on arrival in the UK. However, we advise non-visa nationals to apply for Entry Clearance before coming to the UK to avoid any problems, such as a visa refusal at the airport. Please be aware that you may be refused entry if you do not have a valid visa for the UK.
Furthermore, we strongly advise you to apply for a visa before travelling to the UK if: you have any unspent criminal convictions in any country, including the UK; you have previously been refused entry, deported or otherwise removed from the UK; you have breached the terms of any previous entry to the UK (for example, by working illegally or staying here after your permission to stay expired); you have previously applied for a visa and been refused; or you have been warned by a UK official that you should obtain a visa before you travel to the UK.
Applying for a Visit for study.
You will have to apply online, within 3 months of the start date of your visit, from your home country
Read the information “Apply for a UK Visa” on: https://www.gov.uk/standard-visitor-visa/apply as it will tell you how to apply, what documents you will need and how long it will take.
After you have read the information please select “Start Now”
You will need to:
- Register for an account with UK Visas & Immigration.
- Fill in the application form in English.
- Provide contact email and telephone numbers which are in use.
- Provide details including the following: your address, passport details, your employment status, your financial situation, travel information including previous travel history, course details, accommodation details.
- Pay for the visa fee online (in most cases)
- Print out your form
- Book and attend an appointment at a Visa Application Centre
- remember to upload / take the supporting documents with you which may include the following: passport or travel documents, evidence of funds available to you and clearly accessible by you, evidence of support you will received from your sponsor, including details of what support is being provided and how, evidence of the relationship between you and your sponsor, evidence that you are enrolled or accepted on a UK course.
Please note that this information is for guidance only. You must check the UKVI website for up-to-date visa requirements: https://www.gov.uk/standard-visitor-visa
If you need further assistance please email the International Student Support and UKVI Compliance at: visasupport@bradford.ac.uk

SOURCE_DOC: Graduate Immigration Route_General_Factsheet+QA.pdf

International Student Support and UKVI Compliance
UK Graduate Immigration Route - Factsheet
On 11 September 2019 the UK Government announced the creation of a new graduate immigration route. This document provides a summary of the key points of this announcement for our partners, stakeholders and customers outside the UK.
Top lines
- In the year ending June 2019, we issued over 250,000 Tier 4 study visas to international students wishing to study at our world class institutions – 13% higher than the preceding 12 months’.
- This new route demonstrates our commitment to attracting the brightest and best talent from around the world to study and work in the UK.
- It is aimed at providing greater opportunities for talented international students who will be able to remain in the UK for two years to work once they have successfully completed their studies.
- This new route demonstrates our global outlook and will ensure that our world-leading higher education sector remains competitive internationally.
Key information on the route
- The graduate immigration route will be open to international students who have completed a degree at undergraduate level or above at an institution with a track record of compliance with the Home Office’s immigration rules.
- Successful applicants on this route will be able to stay and work, or look for work, in the UK for a maximum period of two years. Graduates will also be able to switch into skilled work once they have found a suitable job.
- It is expected that students entering the UK in the 2020-21 academic year intake will be eligible for this route. This route was announced in September 2019 to ensure that universities, stakeholders and customers preparing for the next academic year were aware of this change and could feature this as part of their plans.
- The immigration route will be two years long and will require a new application (including payment of a fee) and payment of the Immigration Health Surcharge. The exact fee will be set out in due course.
- The route is non-extendable and does not count towards settlement. However, graduates who find an appropriate job and meet the requirements will be able to switch into skilled work, which is a route to settlement.
- Those who graduate and whose leave expires before the route is introduced will not be eligible for it, however, most of these students will have had no expectation of benefitting from such a route when they applied to study in the UK.
Q&A
What is the new Graduate route?
The Graduate route will provide an opportunity for international students who have been awarded their degree to stay and work in the UK at any skill level for two years. This represents a significant improvement in our offer to international students and will help ensure our world leading higher education sector remains competitive internationally.
Who will be eligible for the route?
Once the route is introduced, those with valid leave as a Tier 4 student, who have successfully completed a course at undergraduate level or above at a Higher Education Provider with a track record of compliance, will be eligible to apply for the Graduate route. The application process will be light touch, and applicants will be subject to criminality, identity and security checks.
Why are you introducing this route?
We have a strong offer to make to international students, and this is demonstrated by the fact that visa applications by university students are at a record high. But we want to ensure we continue to attract the brightest and the best students to the UK. This route will ensure that we continue to be world leaders and remain competitive internationally.
Why are you doing this now?
The Graduate route demonstrates the UK is a globally focused, outward facing nation committed to attracting the best students from around the world. Announcing the intention to introduce this route now will allow universities to promote the route when recruiting prospective students. It will also enable students to decide where to study, knowing they have the option of staying in the UK to work after completing their studies.
What are you trying to achieve with this change?
Introducing this new Graduate immigration route will send a global signal that the UK is open to international students and support our education sector to continue to attract talented students from around the world.
Does the Government welcome international students?
Yes. The Government welcomes international students and places no limit on their numbers. There are more international students applying to come to UK universities than ever before. The introduction of the
Graduate route further demonstrates we greatly value international students.
When will the route be introduced?
We will set out plans for the route’s introduction in due course. Universities will be able to recruit on the basis that students starting in the 2020/21 academic year will benefit.
Why can’t it be implemented sooner?
We are working on a timetable for developing and implementing the route with the Higher Education sector to ensure that it is implemented at the most appropriate time.
Will you cap the number of visas available under this route?
All graduates who meet the requirements will be eligible for the route.
What happens after the two-year period?
Those on the route will be able to switch onto the skilled work route if they find a job which meets the skill requirement of the route.
Will it be possible to extend beyond two years? What would someone do after the time is up?
The Graduate route will provide a non-extendable two-year period of leave. Graduates will be able to switch into skilled work if they find a job which meets the requirements of the skilled work route.
How much will the route cost?
There will be a fee for this route, which will be set out in due course.
Will graduates have to pay the immigration health surcharge?
Yes. As with other categories of migrants, those on the Graduate route will need to pay the immigration health surcharge.
What are the key features of the route?
The application process to apply for the Graduate route will be simple, although we intend that all applications will need to be made in-country and a fee will be applied. They will also be subject to identify, criminality and security checks.
All students accepted for the Graduate route will receive two years non-extendable leave. During that time, they will be able to work, or look for work, at any skill level, but won’t be able to access public funds and settlement rights will not be accrued. Graduates will also be able to switch into skilled work once they have found a suitable job.
How are you defining ‘reputable’ Higher Education providers?
The status of each institution will be shown in the register of licensed sponsors, which is publicly available on the GOV.UK website at: https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/register-of-licensed-sponsors-students 
Institutions with the required track record have the status ‘Tier 4 Sponsor – Track record’ in the register.
It will be easy for students to check the status of institutions before making an application.

SOURCE_DOC: accessing-linux-remotely-from-windows.pdf

IT Services
Accessing Linux remotely from Windows
Information
Linux is no longer available as a dedicated Linux Lab install. It is now accessed remotely from Windows.
You can copy files to and from your Linux session, launch individual applications from terminal session, and launch a full Linux Desktop.
The Linux cluster is based on the latest Ubuntu available for free from https://ubuntu.com/download/desktop 
Pre-requisites
Bitvise SSH client – free download from https://www.bitvise.com/ssh-client-download (this should already be installed in the Labs).
Important note
Linux creates a ‘Vanilla’ (new) user account each time you sign in. This means your work is not saved after your session ends. Any work you do must be copied back to your Windows account or you will lose the work after you end the session.
Instructions 
1. For remote access, in a web browser, sign into Horizon: https://horizon.bradford.ac.uk. 
2. Select the HTML Access version and then enter your University of Bradford credentials.
3. Launch the standard cluster pool from the list displayed.
4. Press the Windows key or select Start and launch BitVise SSH client.
5. Complete the following fields in the Bitvise GUI:
Host: compssh.adds.brad.ac.uk
Port: 2222
Username: Your short username
The default profile for the Terminal is white text on a black background. To use black text on a white background, select the Default colors link in the Terminal tab (above). Modify the background colour to white (255,255,255) and modify the text colour to black (0,0,0).
6. Select Log in.
Note: It will take several seconds before authentication is complete and a new account is created for each session.
Functions
After a successful login, on the left pane you will see the following buttons appear:
- Select New terminal console to launch a new terminal window.
- Select New SFTP window to transfer files from your Windows account to the Linux session and vice versa.
Remember to copy changed files back to your Windows account before your Linux session ends.
- Select New Remote Desktop to launch a full Linux desktop that will fill your screen.
You will see a ‘Check your video drivers’ message, that can be safely ignored. Select the X to close this message panel.
Applications are accessible from Terminal and the menu function.
To exit the desktop, select the Start menu and then the Exit option.
After you close your remote desktop session, you are still signed in to your SSH session and can launch
Terminal or SFTP applications.
Signing out
Remember to copy your work back to Windows first. Then select the Bitvise Log out button to sign out.

IT Services
Adobe Creative Cloud
Availability on campus
Acrobat Pro and Photoshop are installed in rooms 01.53, J. B. Priestley Library and F42, Richmond Building.
Additional software - such as Adobe Premier - is available in Faculty of Engineering and Informatics cluster rooms; however, these are only available to students studying Engineering or Informatics modules.
Staff use
University owned Windows 10 devices
Note: If you still have a Windows 7 device, please follow the instructions for personal devices.
1. Go to the Start menu on your device and select the Software Center tile.
2. Under the Applications tab, select the Creative Cloud 2021 app and continue to install it.
3. You may be asked to close some applications as part of the installation process.
4. Once installation is complete, launch the Adobe Creative Cloud software and sign in using you nUniversity username with ‘@bradford.ac.uk’ appended – for example, jrblogg2@bradford.ac.uk – and your University password.
5. You will then see all the Adobe products available for you to install.
Note: Some Adobe apps – for example, Premier and After Affects - require high specification devices to run and will not be compatible with standard University devices. For an indication of whether the app is likely to run before you install it you can select the monitor icon next to the app name. Please note however, that even if your device appears to meet requirements, the app may still not run well, and IT Services will be unable to resolve this
Mac devices
1. Go to the Creative Cloud website. The download begins immediately.
2. Double-click on the downloaded macOS: Creative_Cloud_Installer.dmg file to begin installation and follow the instructions on screen to complete your installation.
3. You will need to sign in with your University username with ‘@bradford.ac.uk’ appended - for example, jrblogg2@bradford.ac.uk - and your University password.
Personal devices
The Adobe Creative Cloud 2021 package is available for staff to download, for the use of University work purposes only, on up to two personally owned devices.
Note: Do not attempt to download this software onto University owned devices.
Restrictions
- This software is only available for current University of Bradford staff; you will have to remove the software if you leave the University.
- This software is for work at home purposes only.
- The software is not available for students to download under this agreement. Students can obtain a copy for a small, discounted fee directly from Adobe.
- You can only install and activate the Creative Cloud Enterprise Desktop Apps on up to two devices, regardless of the operating system.
- Only applications covered under our current Adobe Creative Cloud Work at Home licence will be available to install. All other applications will give you the option to try or trial.
Device requirements
Different applications have different requirements. Before you try and download applications, check that your device meets the minimum requirements.
Downloading the software
1. Download Creative Cloud from Adobe.
2. Select the Company or School Account option and sign in with your University username – for example jrblogg2@bradford.ac.uk (note this is not the same as your email address) – and your password.
3. Once installed and signed in, you can download the Adobe apps you require.

IT Services
AMOS
Installing AMOS
Note: You will first need to apply for an authorisation code. This code will be emailed to you and will be valid from 1 August to 31 July the following year (to renew your licence, please complete the same form).
Once you’ve received the code, follow the steps below:
1. Download this AMOS installer file.
2. Open the folder it has downloaded to and open the application file.
3. Select Yes to allow the UAC to install, and then select Next.
4. Accept the license agreement terms and then select Next.
5. Select Next to accept the destination folder, and then select Install. The installer will run through copying files to the local drive.
6. Remove the check from the ‘Start IBM SPSS Amos 29 now’ box.
7. Select Finish.
Licensing AMOS
1. Go to Start - IBM SPSS Amos and select IBM SPSS Amos 29 License Authorization Wizard.
2. Select Next.
3. Select Authorized user license, enter the licence code, and then select Next.
4. Select Next.
5. The ‘End of Transaction’ line should display: ‘Successfully processed all codes’.
6. Select Next.
7. Select Finish.
IBM SPSS AMOS should now be installed and ready to use.

IT Services
ChemDraw
The University holds a full licence with Revvity which permits students and staff to use ChemDraw in Horizon and also to install ChemDraw on any device.
Note: This guide is the short version on how to register and install ChemDraw. If you get stuck and need more help, please see this full installation guide (from Revvity) or log a ticket with IT Services.
Register with Revvity
1. Go to https://connect.revvitysignals.com/sitesubscription/
2. In the ‘Search for Institution’ field enter University of Bradford and then select it from the list.
3. Select Register.
4. Complete your details in the ‘New User’ section. Note: Include your University of Bradford email address (rather than your personal email address).
5. You’ll receive an email to verify your email address. Once verified, continue with the registration and set a password (do not use the same password as your University IT account password).
6. Important: You’ll be provided with an activation code – make a note of this.
To use ChemDraw in Horizon
1. If you are studying on a course that requires the use of ChemDraw, you should see ChemDraw in the A-to-Z menu in Horizon (if you can’t see it, please log a ticket).
2. Open ChemDraw.
3. If asked to select a licensing option, select Activation code and then Continue.
4. As long as you’ve registered with Revvity (as above), ChemDraw should open. If you experience any issues, insert the activation code you were provided with when you registered with Revvity.
To install ChemDraw on a personal device
1. On your personal device go to https://login.revvitysignals.com and log in (as an existing user) with your University email address and the password you set when you registered with Revvity.
2. Select Download Now and enter the activation code, if requested.

SOURCE_DOC: connecting-to-the-rds-share.pdf

IT Services
Connecting to the RDS share
Who is this guide for?
This guide is specifically for researchers who have been provided with a ‘network share address’ by IT Services with which to connect to the RDS share.
Windows devices
Note: If you are off campus, you’ll first need to sign into Bradford on Demand (BoD) before following the steps from within the BoD window.
1. Open the File Explorer and then select This PC on the left pane.
2. On the menu bar, select Computer and then Map Network Drive.
3. Select the Drive letter you’d like to use.
4. In the Folder field, enter the network share address you’ve been provided with by IT Services.
5. Check the Reconnect at sign-In box (if it’s not already checked).
6. Select Finish.
7. When prompted, enter your University username with ‘@bradford.ac.uk’ appended – for example, jrblogg2@bradford.ac.uk – and your University password. The mapped drive will appear on the left pane of the File Explorer under the letter you selected in step 3.
Mac devices
Note: The device must be on campus and connected to the network.
1. Launch Finder.
2. Select Go and then Connect to Server.
3. Enter smb:// and then enter the network share address you’ve been provided with by IT Services after this.
4. Select Connect. If you are prompted, select Connect again.
5. When prompted, enter your University username with ‘@bradford.ac.uk’ appended – for example, jrblogg2@bradford.ac.uk – and your University password. The mapped drive will appear on the desktop and in Finder.
6. To ensure the drive stays mapped at sign-in, follow these steps:
  a. Ensure the drive is connected to the Mac device.
  b. Select the Apple logo in the top left corner and go to System Preferences.
  c. Select the Users & Groups icon.
  d. Select the user that has access to the drive and then select Login Items on the right-hand side.
  e. Select the + icon in the bottom left corner.
  f. Navigate to the drive and select Add.

SOURCE_URL: https://unibradfordac.sharepoint.com/sites/it-services-intranet/documents
SOURCE_DOC: evision-overview-for-students.pdf

IT Services
e:Vision - overview for students
This document assumes that you have completed your enrolment processes and that you are a registered as a current student at the University.
If there are any discrepancies, please email records@bradford.ac.uk describing the issues concerned.
Access e:Vision
On the University Portal select the e:Vision link on the left menu.
Course tab
Depending on whether you are an undergraduate or postgraduate research student you will see different information on your Course tab.
- Undergraduate Course tab shows: the photo we have on file for you, a summary of the course you are studying, the award you are aiming for, and your course supervisors and personal tutors.
- Postgraduate Research Course tab shows: the photo we have on file for you, your visa expiry date (if applicable), your registration commencement and expiry dates, the award you are aiming for, and the contact details of your principal supervisor and any associate supervisors.
Contact tab
The Contact tab displays your term-time and home addresses, full name, date of birth and ethnicity.
To change one of the addresses listed for you, select the relevant Edit button – for example Edit Home details. Your current and any previous addresses you’ve had will be listed.
Complete the form with your new details and then select Store.
Enrolments tab
The Enrolments tab displays your enrolments and suspensions at the University. If you feel these are incorrect, please contact records@bradford.ac.uk with details of amendments you feel need to be made.
Modules / Marks tab
The Modules / Marks tab displays the Modules you are enrolled on. You can also see any exam results and questionnaires you have completed.
- To view your results, select View next to ‘Progression / Award Information’ (note: If the View button is not displayed, your department will have temporarily switched this off while processing results. It will be switched on again when they become available for viewing).
- To view a detailed breakdown of a result, select the + symbol next to the module code.
To display all components within all modules, select the + symbol in the link below ‘Progression / Award Information’.
Notes tab
The Notes tab displays tutorial notes entered by your personal tutor or supervisor, and contact notes entered by other staff members.
Finance tab
The Finance tab summarises any unpaid fees you may owe to the University. To see a detailed list of all transactions, select the link displayed.
Learner Support tab
The Learner Support tab enables you to take screening questionnaire as a first step to determining whether you have a learning difficulty for which the University may need to make some adjustments.
To take the questionnaire, select the Complete the Screening Questionnaire button.
If you have a confirmed Learner Support Profile (LSP) you can view it, and any adjustments that have been made by the University by selecting the link.
Research tab
This tab is only available to Postgraduate Research students.
The Research tab enables you to see your approved thesis titles and research milestones (by descendin Academic Year).
Milestones can be filtered by annual progress reports, suspension of registrations, or extension of registration authorised.
- To display a detailed breakdown of the individual research milestone, select the + symbol next to the research event.
- To expand all research milestones, select the + symbol in the blue text instructions at the top of the screen.
- To collapse all research milestones, select the – symbol.
